Nonlinear Projection for Ballistic Correlation Functions: a Formula in Terms of Minimal Connected Covers
Authors: Benjamin Doyon
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
In many-body systems, the dynamics is governed, at large scales of space and time, by the hydrodynamic principle of projection onto the conserved densities admitted by the model. This is formalised as local relaxation of fluctuations in the Ballistic Macroscopic Fluctuation Theory, and is a nonlinear version of the Boltzmann-Gibbs principle. We use it to derive a projection formula, expressing n-point connected correlation functions (cumulants) of local observables at different space-time points, in terms of those of conserved densities. This applies in every d\ge 1d≥1 spatial dimensions and under the ballistic scaling of space and time, both in and out of equilibrium. It generalises the well-known linear-response principle for 2-point functions. For higher-point functions, one needs to account for nonlinear fluctuations of conserved densities and, correspondingly, higher derivatives of local averages. Using Malyshev’s formula for the cumulant expansion, and keeping the leading order, the result is a nonlinear projection, expressed as a sum of products of correlation functions of conserved densities with equilibrium multivariances as coefficients. The sum is combinatorially organised via certain covers of the set of space-time points, which we call “minimal connected covers”. We use this in order to get general, explicit formulas for two- and three-point functions in stationary states, expressed in terms of thermodynamic and Euler-scale data.

Introduction and Main Results

One of the most fundamental principles of emergent dynamical behaviours in many-body physics is that first proposed by Mori [1] and Zwanzig [2], according to which observables project, at large scales of space and time, onto conserved densities [3–9]. The principle says that there is no need to “keep track" of all observables – all particle trajectories, or all spin configurations, etc. – in order to describe what is seen at large scales of space and time: one only needs to keep track of the values of the densities of extensive conserved quantities, such as energy, momentum and particle number, in space-time. This is because on mesoscopic scales, distances much larger than microscopic distances but much smaller than the large variation scales, and in short times, conserved quantities don’t change much, while arbitrary observables fluctuate a lot. Then, by ergodicity – or more accurately, by typicality [10–12] – local observables quickly take, for most times, the values they would take in the corresponding microcanonical ensemble. This holds even in systems that are not traditionally seen as “ergodic”, such as integrable systems, which admit an infinite number of conserved densities [13–16]. This is the hydrodynamic reduction of the dynamical degrees of freedom.

In order to illustrate this reduction, take a d-dimensional system1 with a certain number of extensive conserved quantities, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_i = \int \textrm{d}^d \textrm{x}\,q_i(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}},\textrm{t}),\,i=1,2,\ldots $$\end{document}Qi=∫ddxqi(x,t),i=1,2,…, with associated local conserved densities2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_i(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}},\textrm{t})\in {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}qi(x,t)∈R, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}\in {\mathbb {R}}^d,\,\textrm{t}\in {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}x∈Rd,t∈R. The set of function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf q_i(\boldsymbol{x},t)$$\end{document}qi(x,t) forms the reduced set of dynamical degrees of freedom. Combined with conservation laws, this gives Euler hydrodynamics [4, 13]. Linear response – or the Boltzmann-Gibbs principle [6] – further suggests that fluctuations of observables project onto those of conserved densities. That is, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta o(\boldsymbol{x},t)$$\end{document}δo(x,t) the fluctuation of an observable o at macroscopic coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{x},t$$\end{document}x,t on top of a stationary fluid, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta o(\boldsymbol{x}, t) = o(\ell \boldsymbol{\textrm{x}},\ell t) - \mathsf o(\mathsf q_1,\mathsf q_2,\ldots )$$\end{document}δo(x,t)=o(ℓx,ℓt)-o(q1,q2,…), this principle states that for large ℓ ℓ, we can make the replacement2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \delta o(\boldsymbol{x},t)\longrightarrow \sum _i \frac{\partial \mathsf o}{\partial \mathsf q_i} \delta q_i(\boldsymbol{x},t) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}δo(x,t)⟶∑i∂o∂qiδqi(x,t)inside stationary two-point functions, for every local observable o (for conserved densities, this is of course a trivial statement). This is useful, as derivatives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \mathsf o/\partial \mathsf q_i$$\end{document}∂o/∂qi can be evaluated in terms of thermodynamic covariances (see e.g. [13]). It gives projection formulas for two-point function [4, 7], rigorously shown in short-range quantum spin lattices [8, 9]. Looking at current fluctuations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta \boldsymbol{j}_i(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}},t)\in {\mathbb {R}}^d$$\end{document}δji(x,t)∈Rd, which satisfy local conservation laws \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _t \delta q_i + \nabla \cdot \delta \boldsymbol{j}_i = 0$$\end{document}∂tδqi+∇·δji=0, shows that small fluctuations on top of stationary backgrounds propagate ballistically. The associated normal modes which diagonalise these equations, and hydrodynamic velocities, are given solely in terms of thermodynamic quantities. Velocities may trivially vanish such as in purely diffusive systems, but if not, this Euler-scale theory is powerful.

But linear response describes two-point functions only. For higher-point functions, one must consider nonlinear response, a subject which has received some attention recently in non-integrable systems [17–27] and one-dimensional integrable systems [7, 28–33]. Naturally, n-point functions are associated to nonlinear response of (n-1)(n-1)-th order, and thus one may expect to have to expand up to derivatives of order n-1n-1 in the Taylor series expansion: we should be able to make the following replacement inside n-point functions3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \delta o(\boldsymbol{x},t)\longrightarrow \sum _i \frac{\partial \mathsf o}{\partial \mathsf q_i} \delta q_i(\boldsymbol{x},t) +\frac{1}{2} \sum _{ij} \frac{\partial ^2\mathsf o}{\partial \mathsf q_i \partial \mathsf q_j} \delta q_i(\boldsymbol{x},t) \delta q_j(\boldsymbol{x},t) + \ldots \end{aligned}$$\end{document}δo(x,t)⟶∑i∂o∂qiδqi(x,t)+12∑ij∂2o∂qi∂qjδqi(x,t)δqj(x,t)+…But higher-derivative terms give products of fluctuations of conserved densities at the same space-time point. How do these higher-derivative terms combine in order to reproduce multi-point correlation functions of observables o? Various special cases (classes of models and / or correlations) and diagrammatic approaches have been proposed in the works mentioned above, but a simple and general formula based on simple hydrodynamic principles is still missing.

In this paper we establish this formula. It is based on the principle, or hypothesis, of local relaxation of fluctuations for fluid-cell means of (i.e. coarse grained) observables. This principle is similar to, but stronger than, the hydrodynamic approximation (1). It says that when an observable o is averaged on a mesoscopic lengthscale L, say on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_L = [-L/2,L/2]^d$$\end{document}VL=[-L/2,L/2]d, and taken at macroscopically scaled coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\ell \boldsymbol{x},\ell t)$$\end{document}(ℓx,ℓt), the result \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{o}(\boldsymbol{x},t):= L^{-d} \int _{V_L} \textrm{d}^d \textrm{y}\,o(\ell \boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{\textrm{y}},\ell t)$$\end{document}o¯(x,t):=L-d∫VLddyo(ℓx+y,ℓt) is, for most macroscopic times t, well approximated by4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \overline{o}(\boldsymbol{x},t) {\mathop {\rightarrow }\limits ^{\ell \gg L\gg \ell _\textrm{micro}}}\mathsf o(\overline{q_1}(\boldsymbol{x},t),\overline{q_2}( \boldsymbol{x}, t),\ldots ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}o¯(x,t)→ℓ≫L≫ℓmicroo(q1¯(x,t),q2¯(x,t),…)where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _\textrm{micro}$$\end{document}ℓmicro is a typical microscopic length scale. This is expected to hold away from shocks or other fluid singularities. This is an application of the concept of typicality to the fluid cell, and was first formulated as above in one-dimensional systems in [30, 31], leading to the ballistic macroscopic fluctuation theory (BMFT). It is a projection principle, where under coarse graining, an arbitrary local observable becomes a fixed function of conserved densities. It is stronger than (1), because (1) only dictates how the statistical average of a generic observable o is related to the statistical averages of conserved densities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_i$$\end{document}qi’s in long-wavelength states, while (4) dictates how the fluid-cell mean \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{o}$$\end{document}o¯ of the microscopic observable o, as a fluctuating variable in a long-wavelength state, is related to fluid-cell means \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{q_i}$$\end{document}qi¯ of conserved densities, again as fluctuating variables. That is, not only local observables’ averages are tied to conserved densities’ averages, but also local observables’ fluctuations are tied to conserved densities’ fluctuations. Thus, correlations of local observables are given in terms of correlations of conserved densities – giving non-linear projection formulas. This is, in effect, a nonlinear version of the Boltzmann-Gibbs principle [6], going beyond the central-limit scale to access large deviations. It was shown to give rise to long-range correlations in d=1d=1 in [30].

As we will show, in many natural states in and out of equilibrium, connected correlation functions scale in a ballistic large-deviation form, first proposed in [34] for d=1d=1: in the sense of distributions3on the macroscopic coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{x}_1,\ldots \boldsymbol{x}_n$$\end{document}x1,…xn, we have5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle o_1(\ell \boldsymbol{x}_1,\ell t_1),\ldots , o_n(\ell \boldsymbol{x}_n,\ell t_n)\rangle ^\textrm{c}_\ell \sim \ell ^{(1-n)d} \,\mathsf S_{o_1,\ldots ,o_n}(z_1,\ldots ,z_n), \quad z_k = (\boldsymbol{x}_k,t_k). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨o1(ℓx1,ℓt1),…,on(ℓxn,ℓtn)⟩ℓc∼ℓ(1-n)dSo1,…,on(z1,…,zn),zk=(xk,tk).This defines the Euler amplitude \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf S_{o_1,\ldots ,o_n}(z_1,\ldots ,z_n)$$\end{document}So1,…,on(z1,…,zn). Note how we have traded the fluid-cell means on the left-hand side to a more universal distributional meaning of the equation; this is allowed based on general arguments.4

The combination of Eqs. (4) and (5) is non-trivial. As per (5) cumulants of conserved densities have a specific asymptotic form as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}ℓ→∞. But as per (4), coarse-grained observables are in general functions of coarse-grained conserved densities. Then, using a generalisation of the moment-cumulant formula to “partial cumulants”, called Malyshev’s formula [35, 36], one may obtain the asymptotic form of cumulants of generic observables by writing them in terms of cumulants of conserved densities. This should agree with (5) for every choice of observables, and provides expressions for the Euler amplitudes of arbitrary observables, in terms of those of conserved densities.

We will show that (4) and (5) are indeed consistent, with the following nonlinear projection result for the Euler amplitudes.

This establishes the precise way in which connected correlation functions of arbitrary observables are fully determined, at leading order, by those of conserved densities. It is clear from this construction that, in general, only derivatives of order up to, including, n-1n-1 are required, as expected.

Linear response (3) for two-point functions is reproduced, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \overline{o_1}(\boldsymbol{x}_1,t_1),\overline{o_2}(\boldsymbol{x}_2,t_2)\rangle ^\textrm{c} \sim \sum _{ij} \frac{\partial \mathsf o_1}{\partial \mathsf q_i} \frac{\partial \mathsf o_2}{\partial \mathsf q_j} \langle \overline{q_i}(\boldsymbol{x}_1,t_1),\overline{q_j}(\boldsymbol{x}_2,t_2)\rangle ^\textrm{c}$$\end{document}⟨o1¯(x1,t1),o2¯(x2,t2)⟩c∼∑ij∂o1∂qi∂o2∂qj⟨qi¯(x1,t1),qj¯(x2,t2)⟩c, as well as a formula for 3-point functions first obtained in [31] (by the same techniques), see Eqs. (77) and (78). The four different types of minimal connected covers of four points are illustrated in Fig. 1, giving the following terms (reading the figure left to right, top to bottom), with the numbers in parentheses indicating the order of observables for the permutations leading to different covers:9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & {\mathsf S_{o_1,o_2,o_3,o_4}} = \frac{\partial \end{aligned}$$\end{document}So1,o2,o3,o4=∂o1∂qii∂o2∂qi2∂o3∂qi3∂o4∂qi4Si1,i2,i3,i4+(∂o1∂qi1∂o2∂qi2∂2o3∂qi3∂qi3′∂o4∂qi4Si1,i2,i3Si3′,i4+(1243)+(1324)+(1342)+(1423)+(1432)+(2314)+(2341)+(2413)+(2431)+(3412)+(3421))+(∂o1∂qi1∂2o2∂qi2∂qi2′∂2o3∂qi3∂qi3′∂o4∂qi4Si1,i2Si2′,i3Si3′,i4+(4231)+(1243)+(3241)+(1342)+(2341)+(3124)+(4123)+(2134)+(4132)+(2143)+(3142))+(∂o1∂qi1∂3o2∂qi2∂qi2′∂qi2′′∂o3∂qi3∂o4∂qi4Si1,i2Si2′,i3Si2′′,i4+(2134)+(1324)+(1423)). Note that on the right-hand side of (8), we have, in general, products of distributions. But distributional parts should only occur at colliding ballistically-transported positions, as we confirm in examples. This does not cause problems because every two patches can only share at most one point.

For two-point function in space-time stationary states \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \cdots \rangle $$\end{document}⟨⋯⟩ of quantum spin lattice systems, a formalisation of (8) is shown with mathematical rigour in [8, 9], using the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^*$$\end{document}C∗ algebra formulation. For two-point functions in long-wavelength non-stationary states and in dimension d=1d=1, Eq. (8) was first proposed in [34]. There, principles for the asymptotic form of higher-point functions were also proposed, and fully worked out in [29]. But these principles were based on the assumption that local entropy maximisation holds at high enough orders in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^{-1}$$\end{document}ℓ-1, which was found in [30] to be incorrect due to long-range correlations5. Equation (8) finally provides the full, exact formula for all n-point correlation functions, and in arbitrary dimension d\ge 1d≥1.

The paper is organised as follows: In Sect. 2 we express the precise mathematical theorem, Theorem 2.2 proved in Appendix B, at the basis of the projection formula in terms of minimal connected covers; this is a general theorem concerning algebras of observables and expectation values, independently from the setup of many-body systems. In Sectt. 3, we introduce our many-body setup and review some basic concepts of thermodynamics and hydrodynamics, including local relaxation of fluctuations (4) and the ballistic scaling form (5). In Sect. 4 we explain the main result. In Sect. 5 we give some applications, including the exact three-point Euler amplitudes, which is a new formula in the context of hydrodynamic systems. We conclude in Sect. 6.

A Combinatorial Theorem

In this section we state the general combinatorial theorem at the basis of our main result, the nonlinear projection formula. We consider a simple mathematical setup in which the theorem is proven rigorously.

Let α α be a set, and let \mathcal AA be a unital, commutative, associative algebra over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb F = {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}F=R or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {C}}$$\end{document}C generated by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\textbf{1},q_a:\,a\in \alpha \}$$\end{document}{1,qa:a∈α}, with unit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{1}$$\end{document}1. Let10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \!\langle \cdot \rangle \!\rangle _\ell : \mathcal A \rightarrow \mathbb F \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨⟨·⟩⟩ℓ:A→Fbe a family of normalised linear maps, parametrised by ℓ >0ℓ>0 and normalised to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \!\langle \textbf{1} \rangle \!\rangle _\ell = 1$$\end{document}⟨⟨1⟩⟩ℓ=1. Define the nth order cumulants13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \!\langle \cdot ,\ldots ,\cdot \rangle \!\rangle _\ell ^\textrm{c} : \mathcal A\otimes _{\mathbb F}\cdots \otimes _{\mathbb F}\mathcal A \rightarrow \mathbb F \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨⟨·,…,·⟩⟩ℓc:A⊗F⋯⊗FA→Fin the usual way: this may be done via the moment cumulant formula14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \!\langle o_1\cdots o_n\rangle \!\rangle _\ell = \sum _{\Pi \in P(n)}\prod _{V=(j_1,j_2,\ldots ,j_{|V|})\in \Pi } \langle \!\langle o_{j_1},\ldots ,o_{j_{|V|}}\rangle \!\rangle ^\textrm{c}_\ell \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨⟨o1⋯on⟩⟩ℓ=∑Π∈P(n)∏V=(j1,j2,…,j|V|)∈Π⟨⟨oj1,…,oj|V|⟩⟩ℓcwhere P(n) is the set of partitions of (1,2,… ,n)(1,2,…,n) (in the non-commutative case, every part V in the partition Π Π inherits its order from (1,2,… ,n)(1,2,…,n)).

The main assumption is the following. If so, what values do they take?

Note that the above scaling of cumulants is associated to large-deviation theory. It appears for instance in the context of the ballistic scaling limit of correlation functions in many-body system [34], in the context of quantum simple exclusion processes, see the review [37], and in diffusive hydrodynamic systems via effective field theory [27]. In our application, the variable ℓ ℓ in the factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^{n-1}$$\end{document}ℓn-1 will be replaced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^{d}$$\end{document}ℓd in (15), (16), where d is the spatial dimension (this is just a re-definition of ℓ ℓ).

In order to answer the questions, we introduce the notion of minimal connected cover. Let us denote by C(n) the set of connected covers of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,\ldots ,n\}$$\end{document}{1,…,n}. A connected cover is a set of patches \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Upsilon = \{V_1,V_2,\ldots ,V_m\}$$\end{document}Υ={V1,V2,…,Vm}, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_k\subseteq \{1,\ldots ,n\}$$\end{document}Vk⊆{1,…,n}. Each patch is a set such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\cup _k V_k = \{1,\ldots ,n\}$$\end{document}∪kVk={1,…,n}, and such that the connectedness condition holds: there is no subset of indices K with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\cup _{i\in K} V_i \cap \cup _{i\not \in K}V_i = \emptyset $$\end{document}∪i∈KVi∩∪i∉KVi=∅. That is, there is no subset of patches whose union is disconnected from the complementary subset of patches. For a cover \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Upsilon \in C(n)$$\end{document}Υ∈C(n), we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_\Upsilon (k)$$\end{document}mΥ(k) the multiplicity of the element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \{1,\ldots ,n\}$$\end{document}k∈{1,…,n} within the cover Υ Υ, that is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_\Upsilon (k) = |\{V\in \Upsilon : k\in V\}|$$\end{document}mΥ(k)=|{V∈Υ:k∈V}|.

Let us define the “index” of a connected cover \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Upsilon \in C(n)$$\end{document}Υ∈C(n) as18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \iota (\Upsilon ):=\sum _{V\in \Upsilon } |V| - |\Upsilon |. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ι(Υ):=∑V∈Υ|V|-|Υ|.

Lemma 2.1

The minimal value of the index of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Upsilon \in C(n)$$\end{document}Υ∈C(n) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\iota (\Upsilon ) = n-1$$\end{document}ι(Υ)=n-1.

Proof

To show that the value n-1n-1 is attained we note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Upsilon = \{V\}=\{\{1,\ldots ,n\}\}$$\end{document}Υ={V}={{1,…,n}} has index n-1n-1. To show that this indeed minimises \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{V\in \Upsilon } |V|-|\Upsilon |$$\end{document}∑V∈Υ|V|-|Υ|, take a cover Υ Υ with |Υ |>1|Υ|>1, and choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_1\in \Upsilon $$\end{document}V1∈Υ. Because of connectedness, there must be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_2$$\end{document}V2 that intersects \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_1$$\end{document}V1. Thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|V_1|+|V_2| \ge |V_1\cup V_2|+1$$\end{document}|V1|+|V2|≥|V1∪V2|+1. If |Υ |=2|Υ|=2 we stop there. Otherwise, again because of connectedness, there must be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_3$$\end{document}V3 that intersects \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_1\cup V_2$$\end{document}V1∪V2. Thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|V_1\cup V_2|+|V_3|\ge |V_1\cup V_2\cup V_3|+1$$\end{document}|V1∪V2|+|V3|≥|V1∪V2∪V3|+1. Continuing up to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{|\Upsilon |}$$\end{document}V|Υ| we find that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{V\in \Upsilon }|V| \ge n+|\Upsilon |-1$$\end{document}∑V∈Υ|V|≥n+|Υ|-1.□ □

We denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^\textrm{min}(n)\subset C(n)$$\end{document}Cmin(n)⊂C(n) the set of minimal connected covers. This is the set of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Upsilon \in C(n)$$\end{document}Υ∈C(n) that minimise its index, and that contain no singletons, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V\in \Upsilon $$\end{document}V∈Υ with |V|=1|V|=1:19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C^\textrm{min}(n) = \{\Upsilon \in C(n):\iota (\Upsilon ) = n-1,\; |V|\ge 2\,\forall \, V\in \Upsilon \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Cmin(n)={Υ∈C(n):ι(Υ)=n-1,|V|≥2∀V∈Υ}.Note that adding singletons to a cover does not change the index, so the condition of having no singleton makes the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^\textrm{min}(n)$$\end{document}Cmin(n) finite. The proof of Lemma 2.1 gives a method to construct all minimal connected covers, which is the one explained in Sect. 4 and in the Introduction.

We will show the following theorem.

Theorem 2.2

Consider the context above, and the assumption (15). For every n=1,2,… n=1,2,…: the limits (16), (17) exist; we have20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \!\langle \mathsf o(\underline{q})\rangle \!\rangle = \mathsf o(\langle \!\langle \underline{q}\rangle \!\rangle ); \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨⟨o(q_)⟩⟩=o(⟨⟨q_⟩⟩);and, for all n\ge 2n≥2, the following holds:21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\mathsf S_{o_1,\ldots ,o_n} = \sum _{\Upsilon \in C^\textrm{min}(n)}\ \sum _{a_k^V\in \alpha \,:\,k\in V,V\in \Upsilon } \,\cdot \\ &\qquad \qquad \qquad \Bigg (\prod _{\{k_1,\ldots ,k_{|V|}\}=V\in \Upsilon } \mathsf S_{a^V_{k_1},\ldots ,a^V_{k_{|V|}}}\Bigg )\,\times \, \Bigg ( \prod _{k=1}^n \frac{\partial ^{m_\Upsilon (k)}\mathsf o_k(\underline{\mathsf q})}{\prod _{V\in \Upsilon _k}\partial \mathsf q_{a^V_k}}\Bigg |_{\underline{\mathsf q} = \langle \!\langle \underline{q}\rangle \!\rangle }\Bigg ). \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}So1,…,on=∑Υ∈Cmin(n)∑akV∈α:k∈V,V∈Υ·(∏{k1,…,k|V|}=V∈ΥSa1V,…,a|V|V)×(∏k=1n∂mΥ(k)ok(q_)∏V∈Υk∂qakV|q_=⟨⟨q_⟩⟩).

Although the theorem has a distinctive “large-deviation” flavour (see the next section), the proof is combinatorial. We are not aware of the statement of the above theorem in the literature.

In order to prove the theorem, recall that we denote by C(n) the set of connected covers of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,\ldots ,n\}$$\end{document}{1,…,n}. For the purpose of the proof below, however, a connected cover is a cover \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Upsilon = \{V_1,V_2,\ldots ,V_m\}$$\end{document}Υ={V1,V2,…,Vm}, considered as a set of patches \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_k\subseteq \{1,\ldots ,n\}$$\end{document}Vk⊆{1,…,n} with multiplicities (that is, some sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_i$$\end{document}Vi may appear many times). Usually one does not consider multiplicities, but considering multiplicities is convenient in the proof below. Importantly, Lemma 2.1 still holds on the set of such covers. Each patch is a set (without multiplicities – that is, each patch is an ordinary set, it does not have repeated elements), such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\cup _k V_k = \{1,\ldots ,n\}$$\end{document}∪kVk={1,…,n}, and, as mentioned above, such that the connectedness condition holds.

We now prove Theorem 2.2. For this purpose, we will use Malyshev’s formula, which we recall in Lemma .1 in Appendix B.

Proof

The existence of (17) and the statement (20) follows from (15) and the moment-cumulant formula (14): in this formula, the single dominant term as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}ℓ→∞ is that which is fully factorised (if the state comes from a probability measure, one may simply use the Bienaymé-Chebyshev inequality).

We note that (21) is multilinear in the observable (algebra element) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf o(\underline{ q})$$\end{document}o(q_). Hence, if (21) holds for all variables of the monomial, centered form:22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathsf o(\underline{ q}) = \delta q_{a_{1}}\cdots \delta q_{a_{r}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}o(q_)=δqa1⋯δqarwith \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta q_a = q_a - \langle \!\langle q_a(z)\rangle \!\rangle \,\textbf{1}$$\end{document}δqa=qa-⟨⟨qa(z)⟩⟩1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \!\langle q_a(z)\rangle \!\rangle =\lim _{\ell \rightarrow \infty } \langle \!\langle q_a\rangle \!\rangle _\ell $$\end{document}⟨⟨qa(z)⟩⟩=limℓ→∞⟨⟨qa⟩⟩ℓ, then it holds for all observables.

Consider23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathsf o_k(\underline{ q}) = \delta q_{a_{k1}}\cdots \delta q_{a_{kr_k}},\quad k=1,2,\ldots ,n \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ok(q_)=δqak1⋯δqakrk,k=1,2,…,nfor algebra element indices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{kl}\in \alpha $$\end{document}akl∈α parametrised by two indices k, l, where the range \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l=1,\ldots ,r_k$$\end{document}l=1,…,rk may depend on k. We use (134) (Appendix B) in order to evaluate\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy}
