Also, we replace m by n in (134) in order to agree with the choice of variable made in (21) (that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \{1,\ldots ,n\}$$\end{document}k∈{1,…,n}). We take n\ge 2n≥2.

Every partially connected partition Π Π in the sum in (134) gives rise to a connected cover \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Upsilon \in C(n)$$\end{document}Υ∈C(n) of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,\ldots ,n\}$$\end{document}{1,…,n}. This is done as follows. We associate to every part\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ W=\{(k_1,l_1),\ldots ,(k_{|W|},l_{|W|})\}\in \Pi $$\end{document}W={(k1,l1),…,(k|W|,l|W|)}∈Πin the partition, the patch \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^\sharp = \{k_1,\ldots ,k_{|V|}\}\in \Upsilon $$\end{document}V♯={k1,…,k|V|}∈Υ. This is the set associated to the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V=V(W):=(k_1,\ldots ,k_{|V|})$$\end{document}V=V(W):=(k1,…,k|V|) (with |V|=|W||V|=|W|), where we discount possible multiplicities – a particular value may be repeated in the sequence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_s=k_{s'}$$\end{document}ks=ks′ for some s≠ s's≠s′ (in this case, we necessarily have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l_s\ne l_{s'}$$\end{document}ls≠ls′), but if this happens, we put this value only once in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^\sharp $$\end{document}V♯. For definiteness, the sequence is specified, say, by the non-decreasing order, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_s\le k_{s+1}$$\end{document}ks≤ks+1. The fact that this gives rise to a connected cover, is because every partition is such that the set of patches covers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,\ldots ,n\}$$\end{document}{1,…,n} by definition of a partition, that is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\cup _{W\in \Pi } V(W)^\sharp = \{1,\ldots ,n\}$$\end{document}∪W∈ΠV(W)♯={1,…,n}; and the connectedness condition of the partition implies the connectedness condition of the cover. As per (134), we associate to every part W the factor\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \langle \!\langle \delta q_{a_{k_1l_1}} ,\ldots , \delta q_{a_{k_{|V|}l_{|V|}}}\rangle \!\rangle ^\textrm{c}_\ell , $$\end{document}⟨⟨δqak1l1,…,δqak|V|l|V|⟩⟩ℓc,over which we take the product for a given partition. Thus, we have to sum such products, over all connected covers Υ Υ that are associated to connected partitions.

In this sum over covers, a given cover Υ Υ may occur multiple times, as any given cover may be associated to many partitions, because of the freedom on the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l_s$$\end{document}ls’s. Further, Υ Υ is itself a set (of patches) with multiplicities, as different parts W in a partition may lead to the same patch \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V(W)^\sharp $$\end{document}V(W)♯. Given a patch \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^\sharp \in \Upsilon $$\end{document}V♯∈Υ, the choice of sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V=(k_1,\ldots ,k_{|V|})$$\end{document}V=(k1,…,k|V|) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=(l_1,\ldots ,l_{|V|})$$\end{document}L=(l1,…,l|V|) depends on the copy of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^\sharp $$\end{document}V♯ if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^\sharp $$\end{document}V♯ has multiplicity greater than 1 in Υ Υ. V and L also depend on the copy of Υ Υ if this given cover occurs multiple times.

So we may represent the sum over partially connected partitions in (134) as a sum of terms, each of the form of a product over all patches \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^\sharp \in \Upsilon $$\end{document}V♯∈Υ,24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \prod _{\begin{array}{c} V^{\sharp }\in \Upsilon :\\ V=(k_1,\ldots ,k_{|V|}),\,L= (l_1,\ldots ,l_{|V|}) \end{array}}\langle \!\langle \delta q_{a_{k_1l_1}} ,\ldots , \delta q_{a_{k_{|V|}l_{|V|}}}\rangle \!\rangle ^\textrm{c}_\ell , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∏V♯∈Υ:V=(k1,…,k|V|),L=(l1,…,l|V|)⟨⟨δqak1l1,…,δqak|V|l|V|⟩⟩ℓc,for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Upsilon \in C(n)$$\end{document}Υ∈C(n), where V and L depend on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^\sharp $$\end{document}V♯, and in particular on the copy of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^\sharp $$\end{document}V♯ in Υ Υ if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^\sharp $$\end{document}V♯ has multiplicity higher than 1.

In order to determine which V’s and L’s occur, we use the statement (15), expressed as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \!\langle q_{a_1},\ldots ,q_{a_n}\rangle \!\rangle ^\textrm{c}_\ell = \langle \!\langle \delta q_{a_1},\ldots ,\delta q_{a_n}\rangle \!\rangle ^\textrm{c}_\ell =\mathcal O(\ell ^{1-n})$$\end{document}⟨⟨qa1,…,qan⟩⟩ℓc=⟨⟨δqa1,…,δqan⟩⟩ℓc=O(ℓ1-n). In this sense, the term (24) is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathcal O(\ell ^{|\Upsilon | - \sum _{V^{\sharp }\in \Upsilon } (|V^\sharp |+ M_V)})$$\end{document}O(ℓ|Υ|-∑V♯∈Υ(|V♯|+MV))where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|V^\sharp |+ M_V = |V|$$\end{document}|V♯|+MV=|V|, that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_V = \sum _{k\in V^{\sharp }} (M(k)-1)$$\end{document}MV=∑k∈V♯(M(k)-1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M(k) = |\{s:k_s=k, V= (k_1,\ldots ,k_{|V|})\}|$$\end{document}M(k)=|{s:ks=k,V=(k1,…,k|V|)}| is the multiplicity of k∈ Vk∈V.

Let us concentrate on the “leading terms” (24) which are such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_V=0$$\end{document}MV=0 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^\sharp \in \Upsilon $$\end{document}V♯∈Υ – that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V=V^\sharp $$\end{document}V=V♯ (if discounting the ordering in V) – and such that the index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\iota (\Upsilon )= \sum _{V^{\sharp }\in \Upsilon } |V^\sharp |-|\Upsilon |$$\end{document}ι(Υ)=∑V♯∈Υ|V♯|-|Υ| of Υ Υ takes value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\iota (\Upsilon )=n-1$$\end{document}ι(Υ)=n-1. By Lemma 2.1, any other terms will be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal O(\ell ^{u})$$\end{document}O(ℓu) for u<1-nu<1-n. Therefore, we have shown that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf S_{o_1,\ldots ,o_n}$$\end{document}So1,…,on, defined as the limit Eq. (16), exists for all n\ge 2n≥2 (the case n=1n=1 was already shown), and that these other terms give zero contribution to this limit.

For the leading terms, multiplicities of elements in V are all M(k)=1M(k)=1. Hence any singleton \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V\in \Upsilon \in C(n)$$\end{document}V∈Υ∈C(n) is associated with a factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \!\langle \delta q_{a_{kl}}\rangle \!\rangle _\ell $$\end{document}⟨⟨δqakl⟩⟩ℓ which vanishes in the limit, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lim _{\ell \rightarrow \infty } \langle \!\langle \delta q_{a_{kl}}\rangle \!\rangle _\ell =0$$\end{document}limℓ→∞⟨⟨δqakl⟩⟩ℓ=0, by centering in (23). Thus, we are left with connected covers which have no singletons, and with index n-1n-1. These are the minimal connected covers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^\textrm{min}(n)$$\end{document}Cmin(n).

Taking the limit, there is some subset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^\sharp \subseteq C^\textrm{min}(n)$$\end{document}C♯⊆Cmin(n) such that, adjoining appropriate multiplicities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^\sharp \rightarrow C$$\end{document}C♯→C, we have25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathsf S_{o_1,\ldots ,o_n} = \sum _{\Upsilon \in C} \prod _{\begin{array}{c} V=\{k_1,\ldots ,k_{|V|}\}\in \Upsilon \\ (l_1,\ldots ,l_{|V|}) \end{array}} \mathsf S_{a_{k_1l_1},\ldots , a_{k_{|V|}l_{|V|}}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}So1,…,on=∑Υ∈C∏V={k1,…,k|V|}∈Υ(l1,…,l|V|)Sak1l1,…,ak|V|l|V|.Because the cover Υ Υ is minimal, its elements have no multiplicities: each set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V\in \Upsilon $$\end{document}V∈Υ is present only once, and hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(l_1,\ldots ,l_{|V|})$$\end{document}(l1,…,l|V|) is a sequence that is a function of V. But a cover \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Upsilon \in C$$\end{document}Υ∈C may be present multiple times (may have multiplicity greater than 1), hence give rise to many terms, and the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V\mapsto L=(l_1,\ldots ,l_{|V|})$$\end{document}V↦L=(l1,…,l|V|) depends on the copy of the cover taken.

Finally, we have to determine the exact set of partitions associated to each cover \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Upsilon \in C^\textrm{min}(n)$$\end{document}Υ∈Cmin(n). For a given patch \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V=\{k_1,\ldots ,k_{|V|}\}\in \Upsilon $$\end{document}V={k1,…,k|V|}∈Υ, the set of all possible associated parts, in the partition Γ Γ giving rise to this cover, are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W=\{(k_1,l_1),\ldots ,(k_{|V|},l_{|V|})\}$$\end{document}W={(k1,l1),…,(k|V|,l|V|)} with all choices of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=(l_1,\ldots , l_{|V|})$$\end{document}L=(l1,…,l|V|) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l_s \in \{1,\ldots ,r_{k_j}\}$$\end{document}ls∈{1,…,rkj}, with the sole constraint that any element k common to a certain number of patches, must come, on the different patches, with different values of l’s. These certainly cover all the possible partitions Γ Γ, and, importantly, do not overcount them, because, as we have established, no two V’s are equal (so there are no two choices of sequences L that would just interchange two such V’s).

Note that the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=(l_1,\ldots , l_{|V|})$$\end{document}L=(l1,…,l|V|) is unambiguous from W: we have the non-decreasing condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_s\le k_{s+1}$$\end{document}ks≤ks+1, and as V is a set, this becomes the strictly increasing condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_s< k_{s+1}$$\end{document}ks<ks+1, thus uniquely fixing what we mean by the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l_1,\ldots ,l_{|V|}$$\end{document}l1,…,l|V|. This gives, for every V, a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l^V:k\in V\rightarrow \{1,\ldots ,r_k\}$$\end{document}lV:k∈V→{1,…,rk} with the constraint \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l^V(k)\ne l^{V'}(k)$$\end{document}lV(k)≠l′(k) for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V,V'\in \Upsilon _k$$\end{document}V,V′∈Υk, where we recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Upsilon _k = \{V\in \Upsilon : k\in V\}$$\end{document}Υk={V∈Υ:k∈V}.

In order to obtain the final formula, we must translate this description, which is in terms of (k, l), into a description in terms of algebra element index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a\in \alpha $$\end{document}a∈α. This is done by using derivatives of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf o_k(\underline{\mathsf q})$$\end{document}ok(q_) evaluated at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf q_a = \langle \!\langle q_a\rangle \!\rangle $$\end{document}qa=⟨⟨qa⟩⟩. Indeed, by the centering (22), these derivatives vanish unless all factors present have been differentiated, and this implements the constraint above. However, we must be careful to count appropriately, because indices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{kl}$$\end{document}akl may repeat. We do this as follows.

Given k, consider the equivalence relation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l\sim _k l'$$\end{document}l∼kl′ defined by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{kl}=a_{kl'}$$\end{document}akl=akl′. We define the symmetry factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_k$$\end{document}sk as the following product over distinct equivalence classes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[l]_k = \{l':l'\sim _k l\}$$\end{document}[l]k={l′:l′∼kl}:26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} s_k = \prod _{[l]_k} |[l]_k|!\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}sk=∏[l]k|[l]k|!.Recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_\Upsilon (k) = |\Upsilon _k|$$\end{document}mΥ(k)=|Υk| is the number of times k is included within a patch \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V\in \Upsilon $$\end{document}V∈Υ. Given k, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^V_k\in \alpha $$\end{document}akV∈α for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V\in \Upsilon _k$$\end{document}V∈Υk. Then27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\partial ^{m_\Upsilon (k)}\mathsf o_k(\underline{\mathsf q})}{\prod _{V\in \Upsilon _k} \partial \mathsf q_{a^V_k}}\Bigg |_{\underline{\mathsf q} = \langle \!\langle \underline{q}\rangle \!\rangle } = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} s_k & (\{a_k^V:V\in \Upsilon _k\} = \{a_{k1},\ldots ,a_{kr_k}\}) \\ 0 & \hbox {(otherwise).} \end{array}\right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂mΥ(k)ok(q_)∏V∈Υk∂qakV|q_=⟨⟨q_⟩⟩=sk({akV:V∈Υk}={ak1,…,akrk})0(otherwise).Note how the factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_k$$\end{document}sk arises because of multiple differentiations with respect to a given, repeated index. For fixed k, given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_k^V:V\in \Upsilon _k$$\end{document}akV:V∈Υk’s such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{a_k^V:V\in \Upsilon _k\} = \{a_{k1},\ldots ,a_{kr_k}\}$$\end{document}{akV:V∈Υk}={ak1,…,akrk}, the condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_k^V = a_{k\,l^V(k)}\,\forall \;V\in \Upsilon _k$$\end{document}akV=aklV(k)∀V∈Υk gives rise to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_k$$\end{document}sk choices of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l^V(k)$$\end{document}lV(k) with the constraint above. As, for each such choice made for every k, the quantity\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \prod _{V=\{k_1,\ldots ,k_{|V|}\}\in \Upsilon } \mathsf S_{a_{k_1\,l^V(k_1)},\ldots , a_{k_{|V|}\,l^V(k_{|V|})}} = \prod _{V=\{k_1,\ldots ,k_{|V|}\}\in \Upsilon } \mathsf S_{a_{k_1}^V,\ldots , a_{k_{|V|}}^V} $$\end{document}∏V={k1,…,k|V|}∈ΥSak1lV(k1),…,ak|V|lV(k|V|)=∏V={k1,…,k|V|}∈ΥSak1V,…,ak|V|Vis the same, the sum over these choices of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l^V(k)$$\end{document}lV(k) gives that quantity times the factor\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \prod _{k=1}^n \frac{\partial ^{m_\Upsilon (k)}\mathsf o_k}{\prod _{V\in \Upsilon _k} \partial \mathsf q_{a^V_k}}\Bigg |_{\underline{\mathsf q} = \langle \!\langle \underline{q}\rangle \!\rangle }. $$\end{document}∏k=1n∂mΥ(k)ok∏V∈Υk∂qakV|q_=⟨⟨q_⟩⟩.Summing over all values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_k^V\in \alpha $$\end{document}akV∈α, by (27) this gives the sum over all possible \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l^V(k)$$\end{document}lV(k). Therefore, the sum (25), accounting for the multiplicities of Υ Υ coming from the choices of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l^V(k)$$\end{document}lV(k), gives (21). This completes the proof.□ □

Principles of Hydrodynamics

In this section, we review aspects of the thermodynamics and Euler-scale hydrodynamics of general many-body systems in spatial dimension d. This includes the generalisation to dimensions d>1d>1 of some of the concepts introduced recently: local relaxation of fluctuations [31], the ballistic large-deviation scaling of correlation functions [34], and the reduced measure on coarse-grained conserved densities [31].

Conserved quantities and ensembles

Consider a dynamical system in d spatial dimensions. We wish to be as general as possible about the system, and thus loosely specify our setup as follows.

The system admits an algebra of local observables. For instance, these are functions on phase space in Hamiltonian systems, random variables of the configurations in stochastic systems, operators on the quantum Hilbert space or more generally elements of some operator algebra, etc. As local observables form an algebra, any finite product of local observables is also a local observable. There is an abelian group of space-translation automorphisms: for every local observable o, it acts as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o\mapsto o(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}})$$\end{document}o↦o(x), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}=(\textrm{x}_\mu )_\mu = (\textrm{x}_1,\ldots ,\textrm{x}_d)\in {\mathbb {R}}^d$$\end{document}x=(xμ)μ=(x1,…,xd)∈Rd is the spatial position. As is habitual in this setup, space may indeed be continuous as in quantum or classical gases or field theories, or may be discrete as in quantum or classical chains or lattice models. In the latter case we replace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}^d$$\end{document}Rd by, say, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {Z}}^d$$\end{document}Zd, and define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}) =o(\lfloor \boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}\rfloor ) = o(\lfloor \textrm{x}_1\rfloor ,\ldots ,\lfloor \textrm{x}_d\rfloor )$$\end{document}o(x)=o(⌊x⌋)=o(⌊x1⌋,…,⌊xd⌋); but this will not play any role here, as we analyse the system at large scales, where space and time are continuous. Further, there is a notion of spatial support of an observable: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}})$$\end{document}o(x) may have finite support around \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}$$\end{document}x (strictly local), or infinite support with appropriately decaying “norm” away from the position \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}$$\end{document}x (quasi-local); below we use “local” to mean local or quasi-local. The dynamics is obtained as a one-dimensional group of transformations of observables, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}})\rightarrow o(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}},\textrm{t})$$\end{document}o(x)→o(x,t). Time evolution can be Hamiltonian or not, and we assume that it is an automorphism of the algebra, and that the system is homogeneous: the dynamics is space-translation invariant (i.e. space- and time-translations commute).

Most importantly, the system admits a certain number of extensive conserved quantities (here and below, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int := \int _{{\mathbb {R}}^d}$$\end{document}∫:=∫Rd)28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Q_i = \int \textrm{d}^d \textrm{x}\,q_i(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}},\textrm{t}),\quad \partial _{\textrm{t}} Q_i = 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Qi=∫ddxqi(x,t),∂tQi=0where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}=(\textrm{x}_1,\ldots ,\textrm{x}_d)\in {\mathbb {R}}^d$$\end{document}x=(x1,…,xd)∈Rd is the spatial position and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_i(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}},\textrm{t})$$\end{document}qi(x,t) is the associated local density (in discrete time, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{\textrm{t}}$$\end{document}∂t is discrete partial derivative). The index i lies in some index set \mathfrak II which may be finite or not; the index may be discrete or continuous but we will use the notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _i$$\end{document}∑i to indicate a sum or integral over all conserved quantities. We assume that there are associated local currents \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{j}_i(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}},\textrm{t}) = (j_{i,1}(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}},\textrm{t}),\ldots ,j_{i,d}(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}},\textrm{t}))$$\end{document}ji(x,t)=(ji,1(x,t),…,ji,d(x,t)) giving rise to local conservation laws:29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _t q_i(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}},\textrm{t}) + \nabla \cdot \boldsymbol{j}_i(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}, \textrm{t}) = 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tqi(x,t)+∇·ji(x,t)=0where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla = (\partial _{\textrm{x}_1},\ldots ,\partial _{\textrm{x}_d})$$\end{document}∇=(∂x1,…,∂xd) (or its discrete version for systems on discrete space).

We recall the macrocanonical ensemble: these are the maximal entropy states under the constraints of the extensive conserved quantities,30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \cdots \rangle _{\underline{\beta }} =\lim _\mathcal {V\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}^d} \frac{\int \textrm{d}\mu \,\textrm{e}^{-\sum _{i} \beta ^i Q_i} \cdots }{\int \textrm{d}\mu \,\textrm{e}^{-\sum _{i} \beta ^i Q_i}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨⋯⟩β_=limV→Rd∫dμe-∑iβiQi⋯∫dμe-∑iβiQi.Here \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\beta }= (\beta ^1,\beta ^2,\ldots )$$\end{document}β_=(β1,β2,…) are the “Lagrange parameters”, now the charges \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_i = \int _\mathcal {V} \textrm{d}^d \textrm{x}\,q_i(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}})$$\end{document}Qi=∫Vddxqi(x) lie on the volume \mathcal VV, and the large-volume limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal V\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}^d$$\end{document}V→Rd is taken and assumed to exist for averages of local observables. The symbol μ μ stands for a time-invariant, homogeneous prior measure. It is difficult to define this prior measure universally, but, for instance, in classical Hamiltonian systems of particles with canonical coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}_a,\boldsymbol{\textrm{p}}_a)$$\end{document}(xa,pa), one may choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{d}\mu = \sum _{N=0}^\infty (N!)^{-1} \prod _{a=1}^N \textrm{d}^d \textrm{x}_a \textrm{d}^d \textrm{p}_a$$\end{document}dμ=∑N=0∞(N!)-1∏a=1Nddxaddpa (direct sum); while in quantum systems, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \cdots \rangle _{\underline{\beta }}=\frac{\text {Tr e}^{-\sum _{i} \beta ^i Q_i}\cdots }{\text {Tr e}^{-\sum _{i} \beta ^i Q_i}}$$\end{document}⟨⋯⟩β_=Tre-∑iβiQi⋯Tre-∑iβiQi with the trace on an appropriate Hilbert space. We concentrate on regions of the parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\beta }$$\end{document}β_ where the limit (30) does not depend on the boundary conditions on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \mathcal V$$\end{document}∂V, and averages of local observables are smooth in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\beta }$$\end{document}β_, thus avoiding phase transitions. Likewise, the microcanonical ensemble is31\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \cdots \rangle _{\underline{\mathsf q}} =\lim _\mathcal {V\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}^d} \frac{\int _{Q_i/|\mathcal V|\,\in \,[\mathsf q_i-\epsilon ,\mathsf q_i+\epsilon ]\,\forall i} \textrm{d}\mu \, \cdots }{\int _{Q_i/|\mathcal V|\,\in \,[\mathsf q_i-\epsilon ,\mathsf q_i+\epsilon ]\,\forall i} \textrm{d}\mu } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨⋯⟩q_=limV→Rd∫Qi/|V|∈[qi-ϵ,qi+ϵ]∀idμ⋯∫Qi/|V|∈[qi-ϵ,qi+ϵ]∀idμwhere \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ϵ→0 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal V\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}^d$$\end{document}V→Rd in an appropriate fashion [38, 39]. Assuming strict positivity of the covariance matrix (it is clearly non-negative, but one needs to assume strict positivity, which is equivalent to convexity of the free energy)32\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathsf C_{ij}= & -\frac{\partial \langle q_j\rangle _{\underline{\beta }}}{\partial \beta ^i} = \langle Q_i,q_j(\boldsymbol{0})\rangle ^\textrm{c}_{\underline{\beta }} = \int \textrm{d}^d\textrm{x}\,\langle q_i(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}),q_j(\boldsymbol{0})\rangle _{\underline{\beta }}^\textrm{c} \nonumber \\ = & \int \textrm{d}^d\textrm{x}\,\big (\langle q_i(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}})q_j(\boldsymbol{0})\rangle _{\underline{\beta }} - \langle q_i(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}})\rangle _{\underline{\beta }}\langle q_j(\boldsymbol{0})\rangle _{\underline{\beta }}\big ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Cij=-∂⟨qj⟩β_∂βi=⟨Qi,qj(0)⟩β_c=∫ddx⟨qi(x),qj(0)⟩β_c=∫ddx(⟨qi(x)qj(0)⟩β_-⟨qi(x)⟩β_⟨qj(0)⟩β_)with33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathsf C^\textrm{T} = \mathsf C,\quad \mathsf C>0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}CT=C,C>0,the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\beta }\mapsto \langle \underline{q}\rangle _{\underline{\beta }}$$\end{document}β_↦⟨q_⟩β_ is invertible. This defines functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^i(\underline{\mathsf q}) = \beta ^i(\mathsf q_1,\mathsf q_2,\ldots )$$\end{document}βi(q_)=βi(q1,q2,…) for all i’s. There is equivalence between microcanonical and macrocanonical ensembles [38–40]: for every local obsevable o, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle o\rangle _{\underline{\mathsf q}} = \langle o\rangle _{\underline{\beta }(\underline{\mathsf q})}$$\end{document}⟨o⟩q_=⟨o⟩β_(q_); further, the averages are space-time independent, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle o(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}},\textrm{t})\rangle _{\underline{\mathsf q}}$$\end{document}⟨o(x,t)⟩q_ is independent of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}},\,\textrm{t}$$\end{document}x,t. We denote the resulting average as a function of conserved densities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathsf q}$$\end{document}q_, by using the “sans-serif” font for the observable,34\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathsf o(\underline{\mathsf q}) := \langle o\rangle _{\underline{\mathsf q}} = \langle o\rangle _{\underline{\beta }(\underline{\mathsf q})}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}o(q_):=⟨o⟩q_=⟨o⟩β_(q_).We recall that derivatives with respect to conserved densities are related to covariances in the macrocanonical ensemble. For instance, by the chain rule, we find635\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs}
