from which its overlap with the conserved quantities has been projected out.

Further properties of such thermodynamic quantities are explained in Appendix A.

Fluid-cell mean and local relaxation of fluctuations

We now discuss the main tool for the present paper: the principle (hypothesis) of local relaxation of fluctuations. This was introduced in the context of the BMFT in one-dimensional systems [30, 31], but it is more general; it is a particular implementation of the hydrodynamic projection of Mori-Swanzig [1, 2]. This is the principle according to which arbitrary observables averaged over “fluid cells” project, in a sense, onto conserved densities. Here we present an intuitive definition of the fluid-cell mean, which makes our derivation clear; however more formal expressions, in terms of Fourier transform, may be more appropriate for rigorous developments, see e.g. [8, 9].

The main formula is (40). But first, let us provide heuristic arguments to explain how to get there.

Let L to be a mesoscopic length, much larger than microscopic lengths \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _\textrm{micro}$$\end{document}ℓmicro (typical distances between particles, typical interaction ranges, etc.), but much smaller than ℓ ℓ, the macroscopic variation length scale. Consider a region of linear size L; the precise shape is not important, but one may take the box38\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} V_L = [-L/2,L/2]^d. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}VL=[-L/2,L/2]d.On this region, at time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{t}$$\end{document}t, consider the mean of a conserved density,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ L^{-d}\int _{V_L} \textrm{d}^d\textrm{x}\,q_i(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}},\textrm{t}). $$\end{document}L-d∫VLddxqi(x,t).This is a “macrovariable”, on this mesoscopic scale. Because of the conservation law, the time derivative of this is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-L^{-d} \int _{\partial V_L} \textrm{d}^{d-1} \boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}\cdot \boldsymbol{j}_i(\textrm{x},t)$$\end{document}-L-d∫∂VLdd-1x·ji(x,t) where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{d}^{d-1} \boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}$$\end{document}dd-1x is the surface measure vector outwardly perpendicular to the surface. This fluctuates as time goes forward, for generic initial configuration. But fluctuations only occur because of interactions at the surface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial V_L$$\end{document}∂VL. Assuming a finite density, and that interaction ranges are on the (finite, fixed) microscopic scale \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _\textrm{micro}$$\end{document}ℓmicro, such microscopic fluctuations occur because of “interaction events” (such as collisions of spheres in the hard-sphere gas) within the finite-width shell around the cell’s surface of dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^{d-1}$$\end{document}Ld-1. Hence on very short times \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \sim L^{1-d}$$\end{document}τ∼L1-d we expect that the fluid-cell mean of the conserved quantity does not change. This macrovariable can be considered fixed, non-fluctuating: it is a constraint.

Now consider the mean of an observable that is not conserved:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ L^{-d}\int _{V_L} \textrm{d}^d\textrm{x}\,o(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}},\textrm{t}). $$\end{document}L-d∫VLddxo(x,t).This is also a macrovariable, and also fluctuates; however because it is not conserved, changes to this quantity occur many more times on any time interval τ τ. Indeed, they occur, loosely speaking, whenever there is some interaction between the microscopic degrees of freedom (such as particles) within the region \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_L$$\end{document}VL. Again at finite densities and with interaction ranges of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _\textrm{micro}$$\end{document}ℓmicro, we expect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal O(L^d)\times \tau $$\end{document}O(Ld)×τ independent fluctuations to occur in time τ τ (for instance, again, collisions between hard spheres in a finite-density hard-sphere gas). With \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau = \mathcal O(L^{1-d})$$\end{document}τ=O(L1-d), there are \mathcal O(L)O(L) fluctuations. Hence on this very short time, when the mean conserved densities are constants, the fluid-cell mean of the observable o has fluctuated much more. One can expect, by a “molecular chaos” principle, that the re-organisation of the cell’s configuration over time τ τ allows the mean observable to continuously adjust to the slowly varying mean conserved density, and relax to its microcanonical value characterised by these mean conserved densities. Of course \mathcal O(L)O(L) fluctuations in a volume where there are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal O(L^d)$$\end{document}O(Ld) degrees of freedom is not sufficient in general for relaxation. But over time, locally the system relaxes, so all that is required is that the \mathcal O(L)O(L) times more fluctuations undergone by the mean obsevable o as compared to the mean conserved density, allows it to follow it and keep its microcanonical value.

Here we implicitly used typicality (see e.g. [10–12]): there is no need for time averaging, as for most times, the average over space takes the typical value.

Defining the fluid-cell mean of an observable o at macroscopic coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{x},t$$\end{document}x,t as39\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \overline{o}(\boldsymbol{x},t):= \frac{1}{L^d} \int _{V_L} \textrm{d}^d \textrm{y}\, o(\ell \boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{\textrm{y}},\ell t), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}o¯(x,t):=1Ld∫VLddyo(ℓx+y,ℓt),these arguments suggest that (see Eq. (4)),40\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \overline{o}(\boldsymbol{x},t) {\mathop {\rightarrow }\limits ^{\ell \gg L\gg \ell _\textrm{micro}}}\mathsf o(\overline{\underline{q}}(\boldsymbol{x}, t)) = \mathsf o(\overline{q_1}(\boldsymbol{x},t),\overline{q_2}( \boldsymbol{x}, t),\ldots ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}o¯(x,t)→ℓ≫L≫ℓmicroo(q_¯(x,t))=o(q1¯(x,t),q2¯(x,t),…)This is the hypothesis of local relaxation of fluctuations. See also the discussion in [41, Sec 3.2] for arguments underlying the local relaxation of fluctuations, and how it is modified by correlations of conserved densities at the diffusive scale in one-dimensional systems.

Remark 3.1

Equation (40) is expected to hold “almost everywhere” in space-tiome. It assumes that, on the mesoscopic scale L, the many-body system is in a homogeneous state. This breaks if there is a fluid singularity at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\boldsymbol{x},t)$$\end{document}(x,t), such as a shock, or if this limit is taken within a correlation with another local observable at the same space-time point.

Long-wavelength states and Euler-scale equations

In this section, we specify the type of initial states that we consider, and recall how the Euler-scale equations of the system can be derived. The Euler-scale equation will play an important role in our derivation of the scaling of correlation functions below, and this will also serve to illustrate how the principle of local relaxation of fluctuations can be applied.

Long-wavelength states

The principle of Euler hydrodynamics are most easily illustrated by considering some long-wavelength state, that is not necessarily locally stationary. One may take, as initial state of the many-body evolution from which Euler hydrodynamics emerge,41\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \cdots \rangle _\ell = \frac{\int \textrm{d}\mu \,\textrm{e}^{-\sum _{o\in S} \int \textrm{d}^d \textrm{x}\,\beta ^o(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}/\ell ) o(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}})} \cdots }{\int \textrm{d}\mu \,\textrm{e}^{-\sum _{o\in S} \int \textrm{d}^d\textrm{x}\,\beta ^o(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}/\ell ) o(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}})}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨⋯⟩ℓ=∫dμe-∑o∈S∫ddxβo(x/ℓ)o(x)⋯∫dμe-∑o∈S∫ddxβo(x/ℓ)o(x)for some set S of local observables, associated set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\beta ^o(\cdot ):o\in S\}$$\end{document}{βo(·):o∈S} of space-dependent conjugate fields, and large, macroscopic scale ℓ ℓ. Typically, one takes the set S to be composed of local conserved densities, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S = \{q_1,q_2,\ldots \}$$\end{document}S={q1,q2,…}, in which case the initial state is locally stationary; but this is not necessary for the general discussion. This state is locally translation invariant, only showing O(1) variations of local averages on distances of order ℓ ℓ. It is expected to have short range correlations in general at least for high enough “temperatures” \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^o(\boldsymbol{x})$$\end{document}βo(x) (this is rigorously shown, for instance, in quantum lattice systems [42, 43]). These are just a simplifying features, not essential for our results. For instance, states with long-range correlations may be represented by adding multilinear terms in the exponential, see [44] (these terms develop over time from inhomogeneous states such as (41), according to BMFT results [30] – so long-range correlations are generically present at all macroscopic times t>0t>0).

The Euler hydrodynamic equation from local relaxation of fluctuations

The question is to establish an equation for the quantities42\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathsf q_i(\boldsymbol{x},t) := \lim _{\ell \rightarrow \infty } \langle q_i(\ell \boldsymbol{x},\ell t)\rangle _\ell . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}qi(x,t):=limℓ→∞⟨qi(ℓx,ℓt)⟩ℓ.The main result of this paper, and in particular the higher-point functions studied in Sect. 5, will be a generalisation of this to higher-point functions, so it is useful to illustrate some of the ideas here.

For this purpose, one may simply combine the hydrodynamic approximation of local entropy maximisation (1), with the local conservation laws (29), to get (see for instance [13] for how this is done – it is explained in d=1d=1 but easily adapted to d>1d>1)43\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _t \mathsf q_i(\boldsymbol{x},t) + \nabla \cdot \boldsymbol{\mathsf j}_i(\underline{\mathsf q}(\boldsymbol{x},t)) = 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tqi(x,t)+∇·ji(q_(x,t))=0where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathsf j}_i(\underline{\mathsf q}) = (\mathsf j_{i,1}(\underline{\mathsf q}),\ldots ,\mathsf j_{i,d}(\underline{\mathsf q}))$$\end{document}ji(q_)=(ji,1(q_),…,ji,d(q_)) and each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf j_{i,r}(\underline{\mathsf q})$$\end{document}ji,r(q_) is defined in (34). Now \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _t$$\end{document}∂t and ∇ ∇ are continuous derivatives, as the macroscopic coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}t∈R and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{x}\in {\mathbb {R}}^d$$\end{document}x∈Rd are continuous variables.

But local relaxation of fluctuations (40) is perhaps better justified than the ad-hoc local entropy maximisation (1), and gives more insight.

First, it implies that fluid-cell means of conserved densities are transported according to the Euler-scale equation (as first proposed in [30, 31] in d=1d=1). Although we will not use this here, we explain it as an illustration. Observe that the local conservation laws (29) hold for the fluid-cell means, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{ t} \overline{q_i}(\boldsymbol{x}, t) + \nabla \cdot \overline{\boldsymbol{j}_i}(\boldsymbol{x}, t) = 0$$\end{document}∂tqi¯(x,t)+∇·ji¯(x,t)=0. Therefore, using (40), one can derive44\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _{ t} \overline{q_i}(\boldsymbol{x}, t) + \nabla \cdot \boldsymbol{\mathsf j}_i(\overline{\underline{q}}(\boldsymbol{x}, t)) = 0\qquad (\ell \gg L\gg \ell _\textrm{micro}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tqi¯(x,t)+∇·ji(q_¯(x,t))=0(ℓ≫L≫ℓmicro).Here, the spatial derivatives must be macroscopic derivatives – that is, derivatives with respect to the macroscopic position variables, applied after the Euler scaling limit is taken –, while the time derivative may be microscopic. That is, we may replace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _t \overline{q_i}(\boldsymbol{x},t)$$\end{document}∂tqi¯(x,t) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell \overline{\partial _{\textrm{t}} q_i}(\boldsymbol{x},t)$$\end{document}ℓ∂tqi¯(x,t), but we cannot do the equivalent for the spatial derivatives.

The derivation goes as follows. We first write, from the conservation laws,45\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & {\epsilon ^{-d} \int _{[0,\epsilon ]^d} \textrm{d}^d y\, \partial _t \overline{q_i}(\boldsymbol{x} + \boldsymbol{y},t)}\nonumber \\ & +\ \epsilon ^{-(d-1)} \int _{[0,\epsilon ]^{d-1}} \textrm{d}^{d-1} y\, \epsilon ^{-1} \sum _r \big (\overline{j_{i,r}}(\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon }_r+\boldsymbol{y}_{(1,\ldots ,d)\setminus r},t) - \overline{j_{i,r}}(\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{y}_{(1,\ldots ,d)\setminus r}, t)\big ) = 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϵ-d∫[0,ϵ]dddy∂tqi¯(x+y,t)+ϵ-(d-1)∫[0,ϵ]d-1dd-1yϵ-1∑r(ji,r¯(x+ϵr+y(1,…,d)\r,t)-ji,r¯(x+y(1,…,d)\r,t))=0where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\epsilon }_r = (0,\ldots ,0,\underbrace{\epsilon }_{\text {position r}},0,\ldots ,0)$$\end{document}ϵr=(0,…,0,ϵ⏟position r,0,…,0) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{y}_{(1,\ldots ,d)\setminus r}= (y_1,\ldots ,y_{r-1},\underbrace{0}_{\text {position r}},y_{r+1},\ldots ,y_{d-1})$$\end{document}y(1,…,d)\r=(y1,…,yr-1,0⏟position r,yr+1,…,yd-1). Then, we derive (44) by taking the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lim _{\epsilon \rightarrow 0}\lim _{\ell \gg L\gg \ell _\textrm{micro}}$$\end{document}limϵ→0limℓ≫L≫ℓmicro, in that order. In doing so, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{j_{i,r}}$$\end{document}ji,r¯ may be first replaced by its relaxed value according to (40), and the second term in (45) indeed gives the macroscopic spatial derivatives of (44).

Note that in general, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{q_i}(\boldsymbol{x},t)$$\end{document}qi¯(x,t)’s are themselves fluctuating in the state (41), hence (44) is a transport equation for fluctuating variables. This is the basis for BMFT: transporting initial fluctuations through space-time. Euler-scale equations typically develop singularities in finite time, and weak solutions are not unique. As the initial fluctuations in the state (41) make coarse-grained densities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{q_i}(\boldsymbol{x},t)$$\end{document}qi¯(x,t) rough, Eq. (44) does not define uniquely how to transport initial fluctuations. But this is avoided in linearly degenerate systems [45–48] (see the lecture notes [49, Sec 3.2]), as discussed in [31]. Here, we will not directly use transport of fluctuations, Eq. (45), thus we avoid these subtleties.

Second, local relaxation of fluctuations (40) also implies local entropy maximisation, Eq. (1), without the need for the fluctuation-transport equation (45). This happens if we further assume that the cumulant matrix of fluid-cell means of conserved densities vanish:46\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \overline{q_i}(\boldsymbol{x},t),\overline{q_j}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\rangle _\ell ^\textrm{c} {\mathop {\rightarrow }\limits ^{\ell \gg L\gg \ell _\textrm{micro}}}0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨qi¯(x,t),qj¯(x,t)⟩ℓc→ℓ≫L≫ℓmicro0.Indeed, by (40), we have47\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \overline{o}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\rangle _\ell \sim \langle \mathsf o(\overline{\underline{q}}(\boldsymbol{x},t))\rangle _\ell \quad (\ell \gg L\gg \ell _\textrm{micro}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨o¯(x,t)⟩ℓ∼⟨o(q_¯(x,t))⟩ℓ(ℓ≫L≫ℓmicro)and by the Bienaymé-Chebyshev inequality (here \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb P_\ell $$\end{document}Pℓ is the probability distribution associated to the state (41))48\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb P_\ell (|\overline{q_i}(\boldsymbol{x},t)-\mathsf q_i(\boldsymbol{x},t)|\ge a) \le \langle \overline{q_i}(\boldsymbol{x},t),\overline{q_i}(\boldsymbol{x},t) \rangle ^\textrm{c}_\ell \,a^{-2}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pℓ(|qi¯(x,t)-qi(x,t)|≥a)≤⟨qi¯(x,t),qi¯(x,t)⟩ℓca-2, Eq. (46) implies that we may replace the observable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{q_i}(\boldsymbol{x},t)$$\end{document}qi¯(x,t) by its average \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \overline{q_i}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\rangle _\ell =\mathsf q_i(\boldsymbol{x}, t)$$\end{document}⟨qi¯(x,t)⟩ℓ=qi(x,t) on the right-hand side of (47), thus obtaining (1) and therefore (43).

The BMFT results [30, 31] (valid in one-dimensional systems with the property of linear degeneracy) guarantee that, although long-range correlations develop, these are not strong enough and (46) holds at all times t for initial states such as (41). In fact, Eq. (46) is expected to hold very generally: it simply indicates that the cumulants of total conserved quantities on regions of volume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^d$$\end{document}Ld vanish more quickly than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^{2d}$$\end{document}L2d:49\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{V_L} \textrm{d}^d\textrm{y}\int _{V_L} \textrm{d}^d\textrm{y}' \langle q_i(\ell x+\textrm{y},\ell t), q_j(\ell x+\textrm{y}',\ell t)\rangle ^\textrm{c}_\ell \ll L^{2d} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫VLddy∫VLddy′⟨qi(ℓx+y,ℓt),qj(ℓx+y′,ℓt)⟩ℓc≪L2dwhich is the case, by the bounded convergence theorem, as soon as correlations decay in space, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle q_i(\ell x+\textrm{y},\ell t), q_j(\ell x+\textrm{y}',\ell t)\rangle ^\textrm{c}_\ell \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}⟨qi(ℓx+y,ℓt),qj(ℓx+y′,ℓt)⟩ℓc→0 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\textrm{y}|+|\textrm{y}'|= \alpha L$$\end{document}|y|+|y′|=αL and α >0α>0.

Note how very little of the precise form of the initial state (41) is used, and there is no need to assume any strong version of local entropy maximisation: the state at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{x},t$$\end{document}x,t does not need to have, locally, just the correlations of a maximal entropy state. This is important, because by the BMFT [30], and its expected generalisation to dimensions d, spatial correlations of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1/\ell ^d$$\end{document}1/ℓd develop. The local state only needs to reproduce local averages in the strict limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}ℓ→∞, which happens whenever local relaxation of fluctuations hold and two-point correlation functions of local densities vanish, no matter how slowly, at large distances.

Ballistic scaling of connected correlation functions

Let us now derive the following large-deviation, ballistic scaling of connected correlations functions of local observables. This will play a crucial role in our derivation of the main formula (8). In order to ligthen the notation, we will sometimes use50\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} z_k = (\boldsymbol{x}_k,t_k),\quad z=(\boldsymbol{x},t)\qquad \in {\mathbb {R}}^{d+1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}zk=(xk,tk),z=(x,t)∈Rd+1.The large-deviation scaling of connected correlation functions was first proposed in [34] in d=1d=1, and is here extended to higher dimensions: we will show that the asymptotic expansion has the form51\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \overline{o_1}( z_1),\ldots , \overline{o_n}( z_n)\rangle ^\textrm{c}_\ell \sim \ell ^{(1-n)d}\,\mathsf S_{o_1,\ldots ,o_n}(z_1,\ldots ,z_n) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨o1¯(z1),…,on¯(zn)⟩ℓc∼ℓ(1-n)dSo1,…,on(z1,…,zn)as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell \gg L\gg \ell _\textrm{micro}$$\end{document}ℓ≫L≫ℓmicro. Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \cdot ,\ldots ,\cdot \rangle ^\textrm{c}_\ell $$\end{document}⟨·,…,·⟩ℓc is the connected correlation function within the state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \cdots \rangle _\ell $$\end{document}⟨⋯⟩ℓ of the form (41), and the above defines the Euler amplitudes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{o_1,\ldots ,o_n}(z_1,\ldots ,z_n)$$\end{document}So1,…,on(z1,…,zn) (independent of ℓ ℓ and L). We will also use the simplified notation52\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathsf S_{i_1,\ldots ,i_n}(z_1,\cdots ,z_n) := \mathsf S_{q_{i_1},\ldots ,q_{i_n}}(z_1,\cdots ,z_n). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Si1,…,in(z1,⋯,zn):=Sqi1,…,qin(z1,⋯,zn).Two comments are in order, in order to specify in what sense (51) is expected to hold.

Equation (51) as a distributional equation

The first comment is that Eq. (51) holds as a distributional equation on the spatial coordinates: the Euler amplitudes are, generically, distributions. That is, the large-ℓ ℓ asymptotics holds after integration against Schwartz functions of the scaled spatial coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(\boldsymbol{x}_1,\ldots ,\boldsymbol{x}_n)$$\end{document}f(x1,…,xn). Therefore, in fact, the fluid-cell averaging can be discarded: once we integrate against Schwartz functions, this corresponds to changing the function to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(\ldots ,\boldsymbol{x}_i+\textrm{y}/\ell ,\ldots )$$\end{document}f(…,xi+y/ℓ,…) and averaging over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\textrm{y}|\le L/2\ll \ell $$\end{document}|y|≤L/2≪ℓ, which gives the same function in the limit. That is53\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\lim _{\ell \rightarrow \infty } \ell ^{(n-1)d}\,\int \textrm{d}^dx_1 \cdots \textrm{d}^dx_n \,f(\boldsymbol{x}_1,\ldots ,\boldsymbol{x}_n) \langle \overline{o_1}( \boldsymbol{x}_1, t_1),\ldots , \overline{o_n}( \boldsymbol{x}_n, t_n)\rangle ^\textrm{c}_\ell \\ =&\lim _{\ell \rightarrow \infty } \ell ^{-d}\,\int \textrm{d}^d\textrm{x}_1 \cdots \textrm{d}^d\textrm{x}_n \,f(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}_1/\ell ,\ldots ,\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}_n/\ell ) \langle {o_1}( \boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}_1, \ell t_1),\ldots , {o_n}( \boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}_n, \ell t_n)\rangle ^\textrm{c}_\ell \\ &\qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad = \int \textrm{d}^dx_1 \cdots \textrm{d}^dx_n \,f(\boldsymbol{x}_1,\ldots ,\boldsymbol{x}_n)\mathsf S_{o_1,\ldots ,o_n}(\boldsymbol{x}_1,t_1;\cdots ;\boldsymbol{x}_n,t_n) \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limℓ→∞ℓ(n-1)d∫ddx1⋯ddxnf(x1,…,xn)⟨o1¯(x1,t1),…,on¯(xn,tn)⟩ℓc=limℓ→∞ℓ-d∫ddx1⋯ddxnf(x1/ℓ,…,xn/ℓ)⟨o1(x1,ℓt1),…,on(xn,ℓtn)⟩ℓc=∫ddx1⋯ddxnf(x1,…,xn)So1,…,on(x1,t1;⋯;xn,tn)for f a fixed Schwartz function (that does not depend on ℓ ℓ or L). This is the meaning of (51), and it is why we may express our main result as in Eq. (5), without fluid-cell averaging.

Here and below, we keep the writing (51), which is useful for our purposes. Indeed, a simple way of establishing the distributional equation from the physical principle (40) is to take the mesoscopic scale to be the “largest possible”, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=\epsilon \ell $$\end{document}L=ϵℓ, with the following order of limits:54\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\epsilon \rightarrow 0^+}\lim _{\ell \rightarrow \infty }\ell ^{(n-1)d} \langle \overline{o_1}( z_1),\ldots , \overline{o_n}( z_n)\rangle ^\textrm{c}_\ell . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limϵ→0+limℓ→∞ℓ(n-1)d⟨o1¯(z1),…,on¯(zn)⟩ℓc.Smoothing out the volume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_L$$\end{document}VL, the quantities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{o_1}( z_1)$$\end{document}o1¯(z1) are local observables integrated against Schwartz functions, hence the limit on ℓ ℓ is expected to exist. After further taking the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ϵ→0, the result is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf S_{o_1,\ldots ,o_n}(z_1,\ldots ,z_n)$$\end{document}So1,…,on(z1,…,zn), where an explicit realisation of the distribution emerges as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ϵ→0.

Domain of definition of Euler amplitudes

The second comment is that we must impose restrictions on the domain of definition of the Euler amplitudes, as follows – see Remark 3.1.

First, for arbitrary observables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o_k$$\end{document}ok, we define the Euler amplitudes only for distinct space-time points, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\boldsymbol{x}_k,t_k)\ne (\boldsymbol{x}_{k'},t_{k'})$$\end{document}(xk,tk)≠(xk′,tk′) for all k≠ k'k≠k′. The scaling (51) still holds at colliding points, but extra distributional terms appear. By contrast, positions of conserved densities are not restricted in this way. That is, the distributional equation (51) is for Schwartz functions that satisfy at least this condition:55\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(\boldsymbol{x}_1,\ldots ,\boldsymbol{x}_n) = 0 {in a neighbourhood of } \{(\boldsymbol{x}_1,\ldots ,\boldsymbol{x}_n)\in {\mathbb {R}}^{nd}:\boldsymbol{x}_k = \boldsymbol{x}_{k'}\} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(x1,…,xn)=0inaneighbourhoodof{(x1,…,xn)∈Rnd:xk=xk′}for every k≠ k'k≠k′ such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_k = t_{k'}$$\end{document}tk=tk′ and neither \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o_k$$\end{document}ok nor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o_{k'}$$\end{document}ok′ is a conserved density. This means that all observables, except conserved densities, must be at nonzero macroscopic distances and / or times from each other – avoiding possible “contact singularities”.

Second, the points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\boldsymbol{x}_k,t_k)$$\end{document}(xk,tk) must also avoid fluid singularities such as shocks, both for generic observables, and conserved densities. We explain below why we impose these restrictions. Fluid singularities are expected to be present on a measure-zero subset of the space-time configurations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}^{nd}$$\end{document}Rnd. They may be intrinsic, coming from the initial state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \cdots \rangle _\ell $$\end{document}⟨⋯⟩ℓ, or, we believe, at least if n\ge 3n≥3, extrinsic, emerging because of the nonlinear effects observables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o_1(z_1),\ldots ,o_n(z_n)$$\end{document}o1(z1),…,on(zn) themselves have on the state (although currently we have little understanding of the latter).

The requirement of avoiding shocks is because our derivation of the scaling fails at shocks, and further local relaxation of fluctuations also fails; and the requirement of distinct space-time positions comes from the breaking of local relaxation of fluctuations at equal space-time positions, Sect. 3.5. We show in Sect. 5.1 an example of such contact singularity.

Derivation of Eq. (51)

In order to establish (51), consider the following modified state, which depends on a certain number of Schwartz “source fields” \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _k(\boldsymbol{x})$$\end{document}λk(x):56\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \cdots \rangle _\ell ^{*} = \frac{\Big \langle \exp \Big [ \sum _{k=1}^n \int \textrm{d}^d \textrm{x}\,\lambda _k( \boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}/\ell ) \overline{o_k}(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}/\ell ,t_k)\Big ] \cdots \Big \rangle _\ell }{ \Big \langle \exp \Big [ \sum _{k=1}^n \int \textrm{d}^d \textrm{x}\,\lambda _{k}( \boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}/\ell ) \overline{o_k}(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}/\ell , t_k)\Big ] \Big \rangle _\ell }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨⋯⟩ℓ∗=〈exp[∑k=1n∫ddxλk(x/ℓ)ok¯(x/ℓ,tk)]⋯〉ℓ〈exp[∑k=1n∫ddxλk(x/ℓ)ok¯(x/ℓ,tk)]〉ℓ.This is still a long-wavelength state, with long-wavelength modifications of the measure inserted at macroscopic times. It is convenient to take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _k(\boldsymbol{x})$$\end{document}λk(x) to be supported on a finite neighbourhood around \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{x}_k$$\end{document}xk.

By the principles of Euler hydrodynamics, for almost every space-time position \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\ell \boldsymbol{x},\ell t)$$\end{document}(ℓx,ℓt) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\boldsymbol{x},t)\ne (\boldsymbol{x}_1,t_1),\,(\boldsymbol{x}_2,t_2),\,\ldots ,\,(\boldsymbol{x}_n, t_n)$$\end{document}(x,t)≠(x1,t1),(x2,t2),…,(xn,tn) – in such a way that possible shocks are avoided, and local relaxation of fluctuations has occurred away from the space-time regions where the perturbations lie – the following limit exists:57\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\ell \rightarrow \infty } \langle \overline{o}( \boldsymbol{x}, t)\rangle _\ell ^{*} = \mathsf o\big (\underline{\mathsf q}^*(\boldsymbol{x},t)\big ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limℓ→∞⟨o¯(x,t)⟩ℓ∗=o(q_∗(x,t))where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathsf q}^{*}(\boldsymbol{x},t)$$\end{document}q_∗(x,t) solves the Euler equation away from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\boldsymbol{x}_1,t_1),\ldots ,(\boldsymbol{x}_n,t_n)$$\end{document}(x1,t1),…,(xn,tn). Of course, there is no need to use the fluid-cell averaged observable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{o}$$\end{document}o¯ instead of o here; but this makes the derivation clearer, and does not affect the result7.

Note that58\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int \textrm{d}^d \textrm{x}\,\lambda _k( \boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}/\ell ) \overline{o_k}(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}/\ell , t_k) = \ell ^d \int \textrm{d}^d x\,\lambda _k( \boldsymbol{x}) \overline{o_k}(\boldsymbol{x},t_k). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫ddxλk(x/ℓ)ok¯(x/ℓ,tk)=ℓd∫ddxλk(x)ok¯(x,tk).Exchanging functional differentiations with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _k(\cdot )$$\end{document}λk(·) and the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}ℓ→∞, and assuming that the result is differentiable, we obtain connected correlation functions:59\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\delta }{\delta \lambda _1(\boldsymbol{x}_1)}\cdots \frac{\delta }{\delta \lambda _n(\boldsymbol{x}_n)} \mathsf o\big (\underline{\mathsf q}^{*}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\big )\Big |_{\lambda _1,\ldots ,\lambda _n=\boldsymbol{0}} = \lim _{\ell \rightarrow \infty } \ell ^{dn} \langle \overline{o}( \boldsymbol{x}, t), \overline{o_1}( \boldsymbol{x}_1, t_1),\ldots ,\overline{o_n}( \boldsymbol{x}_n, t_n)\rangle ^\textrm{c}_\ell . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}δδλ1(x1)⋯δδλn(xn)o(q_∗(x,t))|λ1,…,λn=0=limℓ→∞ℓdn⟨o¯(x,t),o1¯(x1,t1),…,on¯(xn,tn)⟩ℓc.This implies (51) by re-indexing observables, and in particular it implies that it holds in a distributional sense (as we take functional derivatives).

Here we make the usual linear-response assumption, sequentially for each derivative, that although the Euler equation is in general nonlinear, it is stable (its solutions are differentiable) under linear perturbations; this is clear from the physics of mode propagations. However, the requirement of differentiability in (59) implies that fluid singularities must be avoided. Indeed, differentiability fails if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\boldsymbol{x},t)$$\end{document}(x,t) lies on a singularity of the fluid emerging from the state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \cdots \rangle _\ell $$\end{document}⟨⋯⟩ℓ (such as a shock), because this singularity moves away under the insertion of source fields. Further, as source-fields insertions may introduce new such singularities, the result for n\ge 2n≥2 (that is, for three- or higher-point functions) may also fail for certain measure-zero configurations of space-time point. A precise analysis of conditions under which it fails is beyond the scope of this paper, hence we take the “safe” position that the result holds “almost everywhere” in space-time.

In Sect. 3.5, we explain why it is convenient to avoid colliding positions in the domain of definition of the Euler amplitudes, even though the above scaling may still be correct; see Eq. (66) and Remark 3.5. However, for conserved densities, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o_k = q_{i}$$\end{document}ok=qi for some i and some k, it is possible to then extend the result to equal space-time points, by using the conservation laws. Indeed, if another observable at point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\boldsymbol{x},t)$$\end{document}(x,t) is present within the correlation function, we may shift the time in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{q_i}(\boldsymbol{x},t)$$\end{document}qi¯(x,t) as60\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int \textrm{d}^d x\,f(\boldsymbol{x}) (\overline{q_i}(\boldsymbol{x},t) - \overline{q_i}(\boldsymbol{x},t'))= & \int \textrm{d}^d x\,f(\boldsymbol{x}) \int _{t'}^t \textrm{d}s \,\partial _s \overline{q_i}(\boldsymbol{x},s)\nonumber \\= & -\int \textrm{d}^d x\,f(\boldsymbol{x}) \int _{t'}^t \textrm{d}s \,\nabla \cdot \overline{\boldsymbol{j}_i}(\boldsymbol{x},s)\nonumber \\= & \int _{t'}^t \textrm{d}s \int \textrm{d}^d x\,\nabla f(\boldsymbol{x})\cdot \overline{\boldsymbol{j}_i}(\boldsymbol{x},s) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫ddxf(x)(qi¯(x,t)-qi¯(x,t′))=∫ddxf(x)∫t′tds∂sqi¯(x,s)=-∫ddxf(x)∫t′tds∇·ji¯(x,s)=∫t′tds∫ddx∇f(x)·ji¯(x,s)thus obtaining only observables at space-time points different from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\boldsymbol{x},t)$$\end{document}(x,t), where the scaling formula (51) holds.

These arguments give the conditions as stated around Eq. (55).

A simple example

It is instructive to illustrate, in a very simple example, how the scaling (51) indeed arises. This ballistic scaling may be surprising in diffusive systems, as one would expect diffusive scaling.

But take the example of a system with a single conserved quantity, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q = \int \textrm{d}^d x\,q(\boldsymbol{x})$$\end{document}Q=∫ddxq(x). For instance, this may be a generic quantum spin lattice, with Q the total energy or, if spin is conserved, the total spin (in the latter case, energy and spin lie in different spin-flip sectors which decouple, and one may restrict, say, to the spin-flip odd sector, containing only the total spin). Typically, the charge density will satisfy, at large scales, a diffusive equation, and the two-point connected correlation function in a stationary state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \cdots \rangle _\beta $$\end{document}⟨⋯⟩β is expected to be that of the propagation of a mode along velocity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{0}$$\end{document}0, with a diffusive expansion of (generically β β-dependent) diffusion parameter D:61\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle q (\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}},\textrm{t}), q(\boldsymbol{0},0)\rangle _\beta ^\textrm{c} = \frac{\chi }{(2\pi Dt)^{d/2}} e^{-\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}}^2/ (2Dt)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨q(x,t),q(0,0)⟩βc=χ(2πDt)d/2e-x2/(2Dt).Here \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi = \mathsf C_{11}= \langle Q,q\rangle ^\textrm{c}$$\end{document}χ=C11=⟨Q,q⟩c is the susceptibility. Then, taking the mesoscopic scale as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L = \epsilon \ell $$\end{document}L=ϵℓ and the limit (54), we have (by translation invariance, it is sufficient to evaluate the fluid-cell mean on one observable only)62\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\epsilon \rightarrow 0^+}\lim _{\ell \rightarrow \infty }\ell ^{d} \langle \overline{q}(\boldsymbol{x}, t), \overline{q}(\boldsymbol{0},0)\rangle ^\textrm{c}_\beta= & \lim _{\epsilon \rightarrow 0^+}\lim _{\ell \rightarrow \infty } \frac{\ell ^{d/2}\chi }{(2\pi Dt)^{d/2}} \frac{1}{|V_L|}\int _ {V_L} \textrm{d}^d\textrm{y}\,e^{-(\ell \boldsymbol{x} + \boldsymbol{\textrm{y}})^2/ (2D\ell t)}\nonumber \\= & \lim _{\epsilon \rightarrow 0^+}\lim _{\ell \rightarrow \infty } \frac{\ell ^{d/2}\chi }{(2\pi Dt)^{d/2}} \frac{1}{\epsilon ^d} \int _{[-\epsilon /2,\epsilon /2]^d} \textrm{d}^d y\,e^{-\ell ( \boldsymbol{x} + \boldsymbol{y})^2/ (2D t)}\nonumber \\= & \lim _{\epsilon \rightarrow 0^+} \frac{\chi }{\epsilon ^d}\times \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} 1 & (\boldsymbol{x}\in [-\epsilon /2,\epsilon /2]^d)\\ 0 & \hbox {(otherwise)} \end{array}\right. \nonumber \\= & \chi \delta ^d(\boldsymbol{x}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limϵ→0+limℓ→∞ℓd⟨q¯(x,t),q¯(0,0)⟩βc=limϵ→0+limℓ→∞ℓd/2χ(2πDt)d/21|VL|∫VLddye-(ℓx+y)2/(2Dℓt)=limϵ→0+limℓ→∞ℓd/2χ(2πDt)d/21ϵd∫[-ϵ/2,ϵ/2]dddye-ℓ(x+y)2/(2Dt)=limϵ→0+χϵd×1(x∈[-ϵ/2,ϵ/2]d)0(otherwise)=χδd(x)and thus we find, in this case, the distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{qq}(\boldsymbol{x},t;\boldsymbol{0},0) = \chi \delta ^d(\boldsymbol{x})=\chi \delta (x_1)\cdots \delta (x_d)$$\end{document}Sqq(x,t;0,0)=χδd(x)=χδ(x1)⋯δ(xd).

Remark 3.2

We emphasise that it is not possible, by arguments of functional differentiation such as those above, to get the large-scale asymptotic of correlation functions without fluid-cell averaging; or equivalently, as distributional equations on microscopic, instead of macroscopic, coordinates. Indeed, the exchange of the limit and functional derivatives in (59) requires that variations of source fields be on macroscopic scales.

Remark 3.3

Suppose in (56) that we use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o_k$$\end{document}ok instead of the fluid-cell averages \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{o_k}$$\end{document}ok¯. Suppose now that we admit source fields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _k(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}})$$\end{document}λk(x) that do have microscopic-scale variations. The same reasoning holds up to (59). On the left-hand side we again have functional differentiations with respect to macroscopic variations only, of the source fields. But then, on the right-hand side, we would obtain not fluid-cell averaged observables, but rather averages with respect to certain microscopic-scale weight, determined by the fixed microscopic-scale shape of the source fields. This would fall within the theory of “structured hydrodynamics”, where the set of large-scale observables account for small-scale structures such as oscillatory behaviours. This is beyond the scope of this paper; see e.g. [8, 9, 50–53].

Remark 3.4

In some cases, the amplitudes are ordinary functions, such as in models of free particles, the hard rod gas, and many other integrable models in d=1d=1 [7].

Induced measure on coarse-grained densities

Another crucial ingredient in the derivation of (8) is the notion of induced measure on fluid-cell means of conserved densities. This is again something that was introduced in the context of the BMFT in d=1d=1 [31], but is more general.

Let us construct the marginal of coarse-grained (i.e. fluid-cell averaged) conserved densities in space-time. This marginal is induced by the initial state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \cdots \rangle _\ell $$\end{document}⟨⋯⟩ℓ, along with the microscopic dynamics: transporting the initial fluctuations by the microscopic dynamics (be it deterministic or not) gives rise to fluctuations of observables on all of space-time. Let us take the conserved densities at macroscopic coordinates. We denote the induced measure as63\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \!\langle \cdots \rangle \!\rangle _\ell = \int [\textrm{d}\underline{q}]\,\textrm{e}^{-\ell \mathcal M_\ell [\underline{q}]}\cdots . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨⟨⋯⟩⟩ℓ=∫[dq_]e-ℓMℓ[q_]⋯.That is,64\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \overline{q_{i_1}}( z_1)\cdots \overline{q_{i_n}}( z_n)\rangle _\ell = \langle \!\langle q_{i_1}(z_1)\cdots q_{i_n}(z_n)\rangle \!\rangle _\ell \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨qi1¯(z1)⋯qin¯(zn)⟩ℓ=⟨⟨qi1(z1)⋯qin(zn)⟩⟩ℓfor all ℓ ,L,nℓ,L,n and all choices of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_k,\,z_k=(\boldsymbol{x}_k,t_k)$$\end{document}ik,zk=(xk,tk). We write the induce measure in the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\textrm{d}\underline{q}]\,\textrm{e}^{-\ell \mathcal M_\ell [\underline{q}]}$$\end{document}[dq_]e-ℓMℓ[q_], where formally \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\textrm{d}\underline{q}] = \prod _{\boldsymbol{x},t,i} q_i(\boldsymbol{x},t)$$\end{document}[dq_]=∏x,t,iqi(x,t), and where the extra factor ℓ ℓ in the exponential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{e}^{-\ell \mathcal M_\ell [\underline{q}]}$$\end{document}e-ℓMℓ[q_] is there for a reason that will become clear below. In fact, the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal M_\ell [\underline{q}]$$\end{document}Mℓ[q_] also depends on L, but we keep it implicit.

We would like to take the macroscopic limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell \gg L\gg \ell _\textrm{micro}$$\end{document}ℓ≫L≫ℓmicro on this measure. But what information remains in this limit?

We will argue that the induced measure (63) leads to the following behaviour of connected correlation functions in that limit:65\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \!\langle q_{i_1}(z_1),\ldots ,q_{i_n}(z_n)\rangle \!\rangle _\ell ^\textrm{c} \sim \ell ^{(1-n)d}\mathsf S_{i_1,\ldots ,i_n}(z_1,\cdots ,z_n) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨⟨qi1(z1),…,qin(zn)⟩⟩ℓc∼ℓ(1-n)dSi1,…,in(z1,⋯,zn)and, away from fluid singularities and if further \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_j\ne z_{j'}$$\end{document}zj≠zj′ for all j≠ j'j≠j′,66\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \!\langle \mathsf o_1(\underline{q}(z_1)),\ldots ,\mathsf o_n(\underline{q}(z_n))\rangle \!\rangle _\ell ^\textrm{c}\sim \ell ^{(1-n)d}\mathsf S_{o_1,\ldots ,o_n}(z_1,\cdots ,z_n). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨⟨o1(q_(z1)),…,on(q_(zn))⟩⟩ℓc∼ℓ(1-n)dSo1,…,on(z1,⋯,zn).That is, the induced measure (63) on coarse-grained conserved densities is such that the large-ℓ ℓ leading asymptotics it gives rise to for the connected correlation functions of densities equals that obtained in the original state, and that the leading asymptotics for the random variables given by the microcanonical averages of local observables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf o(\underline{q})$$\end{document}o(q_), equals that of these observables in the original state. These are the main relations that will allow us to deduce the projection formula.

For relation (66), it is crucial that fluid singularities be avoided (see Remark 3.1), and that coordinates be distinct (see Remark 3.5 below), otherwise it is not expected to hold. The latter is the reason for imposing this constraint on the domain of the Euler amplitude, as described around Eq. (55).

Relation (65) is directly a consequence of (51) applied to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o_k = q_{i_k}$$\end{document}ok=qik, and the definition of the induced measure, Eq. (64). But relation (66) is non-trivial. Let us now argue for it.

The scaling (65) is of large-deviation type, and implies that there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F[\underline{\beta }]$$\end{document}F[β_] such that67\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Big \langle \!\!\Big \langle \exp \Big [\ell \sum _i \int \textrm{d}^{d+1} z\,\lambda ^i(z) q_{i}( z)\Big ]\Big \rangle \!\!\Big \rangle _\ell \asymp \exp \ell F[\underline{\beta }]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}〈〈exp[ℓ∑i∫dd+1zλi(z)qi(z)]〉〉ℓ≍expℓF[β_].Indeed, (67) is equivalent to saying that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F[\underline{\beta }]$$\end{document}F[β_] is the generating functions of the Euler-scale amplitudes of conserved densities,68\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F[\underline{\beta }] = \sum _i\int \textrm{d}^{d+1} z\,\lambda ^i(z) \mathsf q_i(z)+ \frac{1}{2} \sum _{i_1,i_2}\int \textrm{d}^{d+1} z_1\int \textrm{d}^{d+1} z_2\,\lambda ^{i_1}(z_1) \lambda ^{i_2}(z_2) \mathsf S_{i_1,i_2}(z_1,z_2) Note how the z integration involves a macroscopic time-averaging, thus relaxation of fluctuations can be used. We obtain (66) under the requirement of distinct space-time positions, as explained in Remark 3.5.

We will see that the combination of (65) and (66), along with a generalisation of the moment-cumulant formula to partial cumulants (or partially connected correlation functions) – Malyshev’s formula – gives rise to the general projection formula.

Remark 3.5

Local relaxation of fluctuations (40) does not hold in the state (56) at space-time points where source fields are supported, because relaxation did not have time to occur. Thus it does not hold for “self-nonlinear” terms in (71), where a local observable, not time-averaged, is raised to a power greater than 1. We give an example of this in Sect. 5.1.

Remark 3.6

In quantum systems, fluctuations are also affected by quantum effects. However, at large scales and non-zero entropy density (non-zero temperature), one expects decoherence to occur, and the classical induced measure (63) still to be a good description. The analysis of quantum effects via similar principles at low temperatures is an interesting question.

The General Projection Formula

In this section, we express our main result, the general projection formula (8), in a more precise way.

Recall that local relaxation of fluctuations (40) led to the relations (65) and (66) that relate Euler amplitudes, defined via the asymptotics (51), to cumulants in the induced measure (63). Therefore, taking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_i(z):i\in \mathfrak I,z = (\boldsymbol{x},t)\in {\mathbb {R}}^{d+1}$$\end{document}qi(z):i∈I,z=(x,t)∈Rd+1 as generating elements of the algebra \mathcal AA of observables for this measure, we formally are in the setup of Sect. 2, with the replacement74\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} q_a,\,a\in \alpha \hbox { (Sect.~2) } = q_i(z) = q_i(\boldsymbol{x},t),\,i\in \mathfrak I,\,z\in {\mathbb {R}}^{d+1}\hbox { (Sect.~3)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}qa,a∈α(Sect. 2)=qi(z)=qi(x,t),i∈I,z∈Rd+1(Sect. 3).In particular, the assumption (15) holds, with the replacement \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell \longrightarrow \ell ^d$$\end{document}ℓ⟶ℓd. Therefore, the general result (21) should hold.

There are a few subtleties in using (21). First, we have only defined Euler amplitudes for local observables9o(z), which, we have shown, map to functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf o(\underline{q}(z))$$\end{document}o(q_(z)) for cumulants in the measure (63) when positions are distinct and away from fluid singularities. In particular, these are not generic elements of the algebra, which also includes products of such local functions at different points. Thus we only take a special case of the more general formula (21):75\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathsf o_k(\underline{q}) \hbox { (Sect.~2) } \text{ is } \text{ restricted } \text{ to } \mathsf o_k(\underline{q}(z_k))\hbox { at distinct positions } z_k \hbox { (Sect.~3)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ok(q_)(Sect. 2)isrestrictedtook(q_(zk))at distinct positionszk(Sect. 3).Second, Euler amplitudes are, in general, distributions. This makes the application of (21) more subtle, see Remark 4.1 and the discussion below. We now express the result within the present context, with these restrictions.

Recall the concept of minimal connected covers of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,\ldots ,n\}$$\end{document}{1,…,n}, from Sect. 2, which we denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^\textrm{min}(n)$$\end{document}Cmin(n). Comparing with the result in the Introduction, here we think of covers of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,\ldots ,n\}$$\end{document}{1,…,n}, instead of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{z_1,\ldots ,z_n\}$$\end{document}{z1,…,zn}, but this is equivalent. Recall that a cover \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Upsilon = \{V_1,V_2,\ldots ,V_m\}$$\end{document}Υ={V1,V2,…,Vm} is a set of patches \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_k\subseteq \{1,\ldots ,n\}$$\end{document}Vk⊆{1,…,n} such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\cup _k V_k = \{1,\ldots ,n\}$$\end{document}∪kVk={1,…,n}. A connected cover is such that there is no subset of indices K with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\cup _{i\in K} V_i \cap \cup _{i\not \in K}V_i = \emptyset $$\end{document}∪i∈KVi∩∪i∉KVi=∅. So all patches are, in a sense, connected; it is possible to “walk" between any two elements of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,\ldots ,n\}$$\end{document}{1,…,n}, with the rule that at every step, from one element to another, the two elements must belong to a common patch. Minimality requires, however, that they overlap the least possible, and that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|V_k|\ge 2$$\end{document}|Vk|≥2, avoiding singletons.

Minimal connected covers are define in Eq. (19), Sect. 2. Here we express how to construct all minimal connected covers. In formula (21), for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V\in \Upsilon $$\end{document}V∈Υ and k∈ Vk∈V, we construct an independent, free index variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i^{V}_{k}\in \mathfrak I$$\end{document}ikV∈I that runs over the set of conserved quantities of the model; so each index variable is itself indexed by the patch V, and the element k∈ Vk∈V within the patch. Then Eq.
