Equation (76) is a formula for the Euler-scale amplitudes. On the left-hand side, multiplying by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^{(1-n)d}$$\end{document}ℓ(1-n)d, we obtain the leading asymptotic of the physical correlation function (in a distributional sense). On the right-hand side, we must multiply by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\prod _{V\in \Upsilon }\ell ^{(1-|V|)d} = \ell ^{(|\Upsilon |-\sum _{V\in \Upsilon }|V|)d}$$\end{document}∏V∈Υℓ(1-|V|)d=ℓ(|Υ|-∑V∈Υ|V|)d. We show in Subsection ?? that for the set of minimal connected covers, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\Upsilon |-\sum _{V\in \Upsilon }|V|=1-n$$\end{document}|Υ|-∑V∈Υ|V|=1-n (it is relatively clear from the construction method above). Thus we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^{(1-n)d}$$\end{document}ℓ(1-n)d for each term, and therefore the same projection formula (76) holds for the physical correlation functions themselves, instead of the amplitudes, with equality replaced by equality of leading asymptotics ∼ ∼ as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}ℓ→∞.

As mentioned in the introduction, on the right-hand side of (76), we have a product of distributions. Physically, one expects the distributional part to only occur when position, say, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{x}_k$$\end{document}xk, ballistically transported to time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_k$$\end{document}tk, collide with position \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{x}_{k'}$$\end{document}xk′ transported to time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_{k'}$$\end{document}tk′. Thus, this involves two points; distributional parts involving a single point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_k$$\end{document}zk would correspond to fixed singularities (such as impurities) in space-time. As every two patches in a minimal connected cover can only share at most one point, this does not lead to any problem, and the product makes sense. We will see in examples below how this works. However, we do not have general a general proof yet of this.

An important case, where the formula simplifies slightly, is that where one observable o is arbitrary, while the others are conserved densities:79\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathsf S_{o,q_{i_1},\ldots ,q_{i_{n-1}}}(z,z_1,\ldots ,z_{n-1}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}So,qi1,…,qin-1(z,z1,…,zn-1).In this case, thinking in terms of covers of the positions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z,z_1,\ldots ,z_{n-1}$$\end{document}z,z1,…,zn-1, only z may have multiplicity greater than 1. Thus the set of minimal connected covers is obtained by choosing a partition Γ Γ of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,\ldots ,n-1\}$$\end{document}{1,…,n-1}, and forming the cover with patches \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V = \{z_k:k\in W\}\cup \{z\}$$\end{document}V={zk:k∈W}∪{z} for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in \Gamma $$\end{document}W∈Γ. In this way, there is a bijective correspondence between the minimal connected covers involved, and partitions P(n-1)P(n-1) of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,\ldots ,n-1\}$$\end{document}{1,…,n-1}. That is, we obtain80\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & {\mathsf S_{o,q_{i_1},\ldots ,q_{i_{n-1}}}(z,z_1,\ldots ,z_{n-1})}\nonumber \\ & = \sum _{\Gamma \in P(n-1)} \sum _{i^W:W\in \Gamma } \Big (\prod _{\{k_1,\ldots ,k_{|W|}\}=W\in \Gamma } \mathsf S_{i^W,i_{k_1},\ldots ,i_{k_{|W|}}}(z,z_{k_1},\ldots ,z_{k_{|W|}})\Big )\,\Big ( \frac{\partial ^{|\Gamma |}\mathsf o}{\prod _{W\in \Gamma }\partial \mathsf q_{i^W}}\Big ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}So,qi1,…,qin-1(z,z1,…,zn-1)=∑Γ∈P(n-1)∑iW:W∈Γ(∏{k1,…,k|W|}=W∈ΓSiW,ik1,…,ik|W|(z,zk1,…,zk|W|))(∂|Γ|o∏W∈Γ∂qiW)where the free indices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i^W$$\end{document}iW for the partition Γ Γ, run over all conserved quantities.

Remark 4.1

Sect. 2, and Appendix B, give the precise context in which (76) holds, and its proof. The proof is based on the scaling relations (65), and show that (66) in fact follows. In particular, it is important that the first is to hold also at equal points; we argued above that this is the case. However, there are two limitations: Relations (65) and (66) are distributional, while the proof assumes that they are true asymptotic relations. Thus, in order to apply the proof, one should consider appropriate functional statements, where, e.g., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{i_k}(z_k)$$\end{document}qik(zk) in (65) are replaced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon ^{-d} \int \textrm{d}^d x\,w(\boldsymbol{x}/\epsilon ) q_{i_k}(z_k)$$\end{document}ϵ-d∫ddxw(x/ϵ)qik(zk) for some unit-normalised Schwartz weight function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w(\boldsymbol{y})$$\end{document}w(y) supported on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{y}\in [-1/2,1/2]$$\end{document}y∈[-1/2,1/2]. For ε >0ϵ>0 small enough, relaxation of fluctuations (40) holds, with small correction in ε ϵ. Thus the result (76), under this replacement, holds, with small corrections in ε ϵ. If the right-hand side of (76) makes sense as a distribution, even though it is a product of distributions, then the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ϵ→0 indeed is expected to give this right-hand side. If this is not the case, small-ε ϵ corrections may combine with diverging terms as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ϵ→0 (corresponding to un-defined products of distribution), invalidating the result. Thus, with these arguments, we show (76) under the caveat that the right-hand side makes sense as a distribution. We have argued above that this must be the case in general.The proof applies to the set of observables such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf o(\mathsf q)$$\end{document}o(q) are polynomials in the conserved densities. This is in order to avoid discussions of convergence. As the result involves only a finite number of derivatives, one may expect convergence not to be a problem; but a full analysis is beyond the scope of this paper.

This is in order to avoid discussions of convergence. As the result involves only a finite number of derivatives, one may expect convergence not to be a problem; but a full analysis is beyond the scope of this paper.

Euler Amplitudes in Space-time Stationary States

We now establish the explicit form of the Euler amplitudes in stationary states. By our projection formula, it is sufficient to concentrate on conserved densities, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{i_1,\ldots ,i_n}(z_1,\ldots ,z_n)$$\end{document}Si1,…,in(z1,…,zn); recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_k = (\boldsymbol{x}_k,t)$$\end{document}zk=(xk,t) are space-time points in macroscopic coordinates. This is done by solving the dynamical equations obtained from the conservation laws, by the application of the projection formula (80) for current observables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o=j_{ir}$$\end{document}o=jir (with r=1,… ,dr=1,…,d). Crucially, the stationarity assumption means that the coefficients obtained in the projection, the current derivatives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^n j_{ir}/\partial q_{i_1}\cdots \partial q_{i_n}$$\end{document}∂njir/∂qi1⋯∂qin, are independent of space-time, hence can be taken out of the spatial derivative in the continuity equation. Such equations then become closed, constant-coefficient linear equations for the Euler amplitudes of conserved densities. For instance, one has81\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _{t_1} \mathsf S_{i_1,\ldots ,i_n}(z_1,\ldots ,z_n) + \nabla _1\cdot \mathsf S_{\boldsymbol{j}_{i_1},q_{i_2},\ldots ,q_{i_n}}(z_1,\ldots ,z_n) = 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂t1Si1,…,in(z1,…,zn)+∇1·Sji1,qi2,…,qin(z1,…,zn)=0where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla _1 = \partial _{\boldsymbol{x}_1} = (\partial _{x_{11}},\ldots ,\partial _{x_{1d}})$$\end{document}∇1=∂x1=(∂x11,…,∂x1d), and then one may use (80) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf S_{\boldsymbol{j}_{i_1},q_{i_2},\ldots ,q_{i_n}}(z_1,\ldots ,z_n)$$\end{document}Sji1,qi2,…,qin(z1,…,zn). This technique is valid in general, not just in stationary states, but in general the resulting equations do not have constant coefficients – one must input the solution to the Euler equations.

The results established below for three-point functions hold under the caveat that there are no extrinsic fluid singularities everywhere in space-time – see the discussion below Eq. (55) in Sect. 3.4. Indeed, we integrate projection results in space-time to obtain the formula. This is guaranteed at least in systems where no shocks emerge, such as linearly degenerate systems [41, 49, 54, 55].

Two-point amplitudes

In space-time stationary states82\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \cdot \rangle = \langle \cdot \rangle _{\underline{\beta }}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨·⟩=⟨·⟩β_,the structure of 2-point amplitudes of conserved densities, and some aspects of amplitudes for general observables, is well studied. For completeness and as this is instructive, we develop it here from our formalism.

Using (77) and (114), we obtain for 2-point amplitudes:83\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _t \mathsf S_{ij}(\boldsymbol{x},t;\boldsymbol{0},0) + \sum _k\boldsymbol{\mathsf A}_i^{~k} \cdot \nabla \mathsf S_{kj}(\boldsymbol{x},t;\boldsymbol{0},0)= 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tSij(x,t;0,0)+∑kAik·∇Skj(x,t;0,0)=0.The initial condition at t=0t=0 can be obtained by a calculation similar to (62), and is given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf S_{ij}(\boldsymbol{x},0;\boldsymbol{0},0) = \delta ^{d}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathsf C_{ij}$$\end{document}Sij(x,0;0,0)=δd(x)Cij. Contact singularities

For generic observables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o_1,o_2$$\end{document}o1,o2, not conserved densities, formally specialising (85) to t=0t=0 does not give the distribution corresponding to the ballistic limit of the equal-time 2-point correlation function – this is an example of a contact singularity, and is why we restricted the domain of the amplitudes for generic observables as is explained around (55). Indeed, a direct calculation similar (62) gives89\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\ell \rightarrow \infty } \ell \langle \overline{o_1}(\boldsymbol{x},0)\overline{o_2}(0,0)\rangle ^\textrm{c} = \langle O_1,o_2\rangle ^\textrm{c}\, \delta ^d(\boldsymbol{x}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limℓ→∞ℓ⟨o1¯(x,0)o2¯(0,0)⟩c=⟨O1,o2⟩cδd(x)where90\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle O_1,o_2\rangle ^\textrm{c} = \int \textrm{d}^d \textrm{x}\,\langle o_1(\boldsymbol{x}),o_2(\boldsymbol{0})\rangle ^\textrm{c} \ne \sum _{ij} \langle Q_i,o_1\rangle ^\textrm{c}\, \mathsf C^{ij} \mathsf \langle Q_j,o_2\rangle ^\textrm{c}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨O1,o2⟩c=∫ddx⟨o1(x),o2(0)⟩c≠∑ij⟨Qi,o1⟩cCij⟨Qj,o2⟩c.The discrepancy arises because relaxation of fluctuations, Eq. (40), does not hold at colliding space-time points (see Remark 3.5). At equal times and positions, relaxation has not had time to occur independently on the two coarse-grained observables, so it cannot be used.

Drude weight

At different times, relaxation has occurred,91\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\epsilon \rightarrow 0}\lim _{\ell \rightarrow \infty } \ell \langle \overline{o_1}(\boldsymbol{x},\epsilon ),\overline{o_2}(0,0)\rangle ^\textrm{c} = \sum _{ij} \langle Q_i,o_1\rangle ^\textrm{c}\, \mathsf C^{ij} \mathsf \langle Q_j,o_2\rangle ^\textrm{c}\,\delta ^d(\boldsymbol{x}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limϵ→0limℓ→∞ℓ⟨o1¯(x,ϵ),o2¯(0,0)⟩c=∑ij⟨Qi,o1⟩cCij⟨Qj,o2⟩cδd(x).This is a manifestation of a projection formula shown rigorously in [8, 9]:92\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\textrm{t}\rightarrow \infty } \int \textrm{d}^d \textrm{x}\,\langle o_1(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}},\textrm{t}),o_2(\boldsymbol{0},0)\rangle ^\textrm{c}= & \lim _{\ell \rightarrow \infty } \int \textrm{d}^d \textrm{x}\,\langle o_1(\boldsymbol{\textrm{x}},\ell \epsilon ),o_2(\boldsymbol{0},0)\rangle ^\textrm{c} \nonumber \\ = & \lim _{\ell \rightarrow \infty } \ell \int \textrm{d}^d x\,\langle \overline{o_1}(\boldsymbol{x},\epsilon \begin{document}$$\epsilon \ne 0$$\end{document}ϵ≠0. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o_1$$\end{document}o1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o_2$$\end{document}o2 being current observables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j_{i_1}$$\end{document}ji1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j_{i_2}$$\end{document}ji2, this gives an exact projection formlula for the Drude weight.

Three-point amplitudes

Three-point amplitudes can be obtained from (78) in a similar fashion. The result is rather complicated in arbitrary dimension and with arbitrary number of conserved quantities. Here, we express it in d=1d=1 for arbitrary set of conserved quantities, and in arbitrary d, for a single conserved quantity.

d=1d=1, arbitrary number of conserved quantities

In d=1d=1 with one or more conserved quantities, the transformation to normal modes is given by Eq. (121) (see Appendix A). Because the state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \cdots \rangle $$\end{document}⟨⋯⟩ is space-time stationary, the three-point amplitude should be invariant,100\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathsf S_{IJK}(x_1+y,t_1+r;x_2+y,t_2+r;x_3+y,t_3+r) = \mathsf S_{IJK}(x_1,t_1;x_2,t_2;x_3,t_3), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}SIJK(x1+y,t1+r;x2+y,t2+r;x3+y,t3+r)=SIJK(x1,t1;x2,t2;x3,t3),under space translations y and time translations r. The solution (98) is manifestly space-translation invariant, as it only depends on differences of positions. However, it is not manifestly time-translation invariant. As explained in Appendix C, the fact that it indeed has this invariance follows from the fundamental property (129) of the 3-point coupling \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf A_I^{~JK}$$\end{document}AIJK. This arises thanks to the very special combination of terms in (98), including the particular form of the initial condition. This therefore confirms the internal self-consistency of our general projection formalism.

In the case of a single conserved quantity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q = \int \textrm{dx}\, q(\textrm{x})$$\end{document}Q=∫dxq(x), with q and j the conserved density and current, there is no need to go to normal coordinates. The solution simplifies to102\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathsf S_3 = \chi \chi '\delta (u_{12})\delta (u_{13}) - \chi ^2 \mathsf v' \Big (t_1 \partial _{x_1} \big (\delta (u_{12})\delta (u_{13})\big ) + \hbox {cyclic permutations}\Big ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}S3=χχ′δ(u12)δ(u13)-χ2v′(t1∂x1(δ(u12)δ(u13))+cyclic permutations)where here and below, for a single conserved quantity, we use the notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf S_n = \mathsf S_{\underbrace{q\cdots q}_{n\,\text {times}}}$$\end{document}Sn=Sq⋯q⏟ntimes.

d>1d>1, one conserved quantity

The fact that this solution is time-translation invariant follows from the same argument as that for the case v=v'v=v′ in Appendix 1.

Example: TASEP

For illustration, we consider a simple example where there is hope that correlation functions may be evaluated numerically or analytically: the totally asymmetric simple exclusion process (TASEP). See for instance [56]. As mentioned above, these results hold under the caveat that there should be no extrinsic fluid singularities, Sect. 3.4. As we currently do not have a good understanding of such possible singularities, and the TASEP hydrodynamic is not linearly degenerate, Eq. (109) below is conjectural; it would be very interesting to verify it or to understand its corrections, if any.

TASEP is a stochastic model for the dynamics of non-overlapping particles hopping on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {Z}}$$\end{document}Z: each site \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{x}\in {\mathbb {Z}}$$\end{document}x∈Z is either occupied – contains a particle – or not. This model admits a single conserved quantity: the total number of particles (occupied sites). We denote the corresponding density by106\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} q(\textrm{x},\textrm{t}) = 1\ (\textrm{x}\hbox { is occupied at time } \textrm{t}),\ 0\ \hbox {(otherwise).} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}q(x,t)=1(x is occupied at time t),0(otherwise).The stationary states are the product measures, with each site having occupation probability \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho \in [0,1]$$\end{document}ρ∈[0,1]. The density of occupation per unit site in a stationary state is therefore simply107\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathsf q = \langle q(\textrm{x},\textrm{t})\rangle = \rho . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}q=⟨q(x,t)⟩=ρ.Thermodynamic quantities are known exactly, with the susceptibility and average current given by the same expression:108\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \chi = \mathsf j = \rho (1-\rho ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}χ=j=ρ(1-ρ).The velocity and derivative of the susceptibility are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi '=\mathsf v = 1-2\rho $$\end{document}χ′=v=1-2ρ, and \mathsf v' = -2v′=-2. Putting these expressions in (102), we get109\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & {\langle q(\ell x_1,\ell t_1),q(\ell x_2,\ell t_2),q(\ell x_3,\ell t_3)\rangle ^\textrm{c}} \nonumber \\\sim & \ell ^{-2}\Big [ \rho (1-\rho )(1-2\rho )\delta (x_{12} - \mathsf vt_{12})\delta (x_{13} - \mathsf vt_{13}) +\, 2\rho ^2(1-\rho )^2 \nonumber \\ & \qquad \Big (t_1 \partial _{x_1} \big (\delta (x_{12} - \mathsf vt_{12})\delta (x_{13} - \mathsf vt_{13})\big ) + \hbox {cyclic permutations}\Big )\Big ]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨q(ℓx1,ℓt1),q(ℓx2,ℓt2),q(ℓx3,ℓt3)⟩c∼ℓ-2[ρ(1-ρ)(1-2ρ)δ(x12-vt12)δ(x13-vt13)+2ρ2(1-ρ)2(t1∂x1(δ(x12-vt12)δ(x13-vt13))+cyclic permutations)].

Remark 5.1

One may also consider the example of the symmetric simple exclusion process (SSEP). However, even though Formula (102) can be applied, it does not appear to give any new, non-trivial information. As SSEP is a diffusive system, \mathsf v=0v=0, only the first term remains. This term may be simply obtained from the condition that, at the Euler scale, any diffusive 3-point function is supported at equal times, and the diffusive front integrates, under say \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int \textrm{d}x_1$$\end{document}∫dx1, to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\partial S_2/\partial \beta = \chi \chi '\delta (x_2-x_3)$$\end{document}-∂S2/∂β=χχ′δ(x2-x3), where the two-point function is simply \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_2 = \chi \delta (x_2-x_3)$$\end{document}S2=χδ(x2-x3) at the Euler scale. We believe a similar phenomenon happens for higher-point function at equilibrium. However it may be useful to investigate this, and perhaps the more general non-equilibrium situation, and compare to exact Bethe-ansatz results [57].

Remark 5.2

Formulae (98) (and it specialisation to integrable models below, Eq. (112)) and (104) have an interesting structure. The first term is the equivalent to (84), where the connected three-point correlation function of conserved quantities is ballistically propagated along the hydrodynamic velocities. But the second term involves derivatives of delta-functions with the flux Hessian as coefficient. In general in (98) these do not fully constrain the space-time points to lie on the ballistic rays that connect them. This is related to the long-range two-point correlations that are known to emerge in non-stationary states [30, 31]: the inclusion of a third observable creates a disturbance that gives rise to long-range correlations, supported on regions that extend ballistically as shown in [58]. Such effects are also known to contribute to diffusive-scale effects [41, 44], and the flux Hessian is known to contribute to the macroscopic diffusion coefficient [41, 59]. In (104), delta-functions constrain the space-time points to lie on ballistic rays, with a slight extension below the Euler scale, seen at the Euler scale as a derivative. There, the velocity derivative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\mathsf v}'$$\end{document}v′ is involved as a coefficient – this, instead, is known to contribute to super-diffusion in one dimension [59, 60]. It would be interesting to investigate further these connections.

Example: many-body integrable systems

The hydrodynamics of many-body integrable systems is generalised hydrodynamics (GHD) [13–16]. It is known at least in many cases not to develop shocks or other fluid singularities [58, 61, 62], hence for which our general results for three-point functions are more solidly established.

We refer to [13] for the ideas and formalism of GHD. In this context, there are in fact three natural bases: the “ordinary” basis of conserved quantities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_i$$\end{document}Qi for i=1,2,3,… i=1,2,3,…, such that a single asymptotic particle of spectral parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in \mathcal S$$\end{document}λ∈S carries a quantity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_i(\lambda )$$\end{document}hi(λ) (for some set of functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_i(\cdot )$$\end{document}hi(·)); the basis \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_\theta $$\end{document}Qθ for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in \mathcal S$$\end{document}θ∈S, where the quantity carried is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta (\theta -\lambda )$$\end{document}δ(θ-λ); and the normal-mode basis \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_I$$\end{document}QI again for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\in \mathcal S$$\end{document}I∈S. In many models the spectral space \mathcal SS is simply \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} The calculations of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle Q_I,Q_J,q_K\rangle ^\textrm{c}$$\end{document}⟨QI,QJ,qK⟩c and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf A_{I}^{~JK}$$\end{document}AIJK are done by applying derivatives on the \mathsf CC and \mathsf AA matrices, according to their basic definitions. Conclusion

In this paper, we have derived a nonlinear projection formula, relating dynamical correlation functions of local observables in finite-entropy states, to those of the conserved densities admitted by the many-body system. The derivation is based on the principle of local relaxation of fluctuations, an implementation of the projection ideas of Mori and Zwanzig [1, 2] and a nonlinear version of the Boltzmann-gibbs principle, introduced in the context of the ballistic macroscopic fluctuation theory (BMFT) [30, 31]. It holds for n-point correlation functions in d dimensions of space, for all n\ge 1n≥1 and d\ge 1d≥1, and, we expect, for any local dynamics, such as Hamiltonian, circuit or stochastic, both quantum and classical. The formula is expressed as a sum over minimal connected covers, making it easy to work out graphically for any specific n. This generalises to arbitrary n and d various special cases established earlier, including the well-known projection formula for two-point functions, and makes it clear how nonlinear effects arise in higher-point functions.

Using conservation laws and the projection formula for three-point functions involving currents, we have established the form of three-point correlation functions of conserved densities (and therefore, by projection, of any local observables) in various cases, solely in terms of the thermodynamic and Euler hydrodynamic data: the flux Jacobian and covariance matrix. We have shown that the result is space- and time-translation invariant, the latter requiring special cancellations of terms along with a new symmetry relation for “3-point coupling” that we have established Eq. (127). It would be interesting to analyse higher-point functions in stationary states, including cumulants of total currents through surfaces in arbitrary dimensions. It would be particularly interesting to study long-range correlations emerging in non-stationary states, by techniques similar to those of [30]. A natural question is to extend the present formalism to higher-dimensional integrable systems, following the soliton-gas picture where fundamental “particles” are extended objects [63]. The structure of a sum over minimal connected covers is a kind of generalisation of Wick’s theorem for Gaussian fields. It suggests that Euler-scale correlation functions may be re-organised into a non-Gaussian field-theoretic formulation. The BMFT does this for linearly degenerate systems, including integrable systems, however in general shocks may arise, which have to be taken into account. In particular, on trajectories in space-time where shocks lie, the projection formula is not expected to hold, because the principle of relaxation of fluctuations breaks down. It would be interesting to work out the consequences of these on explicit expression of correlation functions.

Perhaps the most interesting next steps are to extend the present formalism beyond the ballistic (Euler) scale (see the companion paper [41]), as is done in [44] for integrable systems, and to include quantum fluctuations, see e.g. [64, 65]. Naturally, one would expect that beyond the Euler scale, the local relaxation of fluctuations Eq. (40) is modify by correlations of conserved densities. On this aspect, we note that in d=1d=1 integrable systems, results of [44] suggest that 2-point Euler-scale correlation functions contribute to the diffusive scale to 1-point functions, 3-point Euler-scale correlation functions contribute to the diffusive scale of 2-point correlation functions, and both of these contribute to the dispersive scale of 1-point functions, etc., as illustrated in this table: In particular, the leading behaviour of n-point functions affect the nth order of the hydrodynamic expansion of one-point functions. More generally, in generic d-dimensional systems, we expect that the contributions at order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^{(1-n)d-k}$$\end{document}ℓ(1-n)d-k to n-point correlation functions, for113\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} k+nd \le rd,\quad n\ge 1,\ k\ge 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}k+nd≤rd,n≥1,k≥0,are all related to each other, but in general with additional stochastic contributions (as explained in [41] for linearly degenerate systems). It would be interesting to investigate and clarify this.
